### 4. ★★★★☆ What Trump’s Waiver of New Russia Sanctions Means for Ukraine - Time Magazine

- **时间**: 2026-10-17T19:17:33+00:00

What Trump’s Waiver of New Russia Sanctions Means for Ukraine
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.
Custom Feed: See the stories that matter most to you.
Smart Alerts: Get notified about major news as it happens.
U.S. President Donald Trump delivers remarks during a midterm rally at Truist Arena on the Northern Kentucky University campus on Oct. 17, 2026 in Highland Heights, Kentucky.
U.S. President Donald Trump delivers remarks during a midterm rally at Truist Arena on the Northern Kentucky University campus on Oct. 17, 2026 in Highland Heights, Kentucky. Eric Lee—Getty Images
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.
Custom Feed: See the stories that matter most to you.
Smart Alerts: Get notified about major news as it happens.
President Donald Trump signed an executive order Friday that waives many of the sanctions in the Lindsey O. Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act, which Congress passed in the late senator’s name last month. The waiver, coming a week after Trump announced a diesel deal with Russia, also underscores fraying relations between the U.S. and Ukraine.
The law, which was set to take effect Oct. 18, called for sanctions targeting Russian officials, financial institutions, and other entities, alongside tariffs of up to 100% on goods imported into the U.S. from major buyers of Russian crude oil or natural gas. Those measures were intended to restrict the financial resources supporting Russia’s war against Ukraine.
The U.K.’s Foreign, Commonwealth & Development Office estimated in June 2025 that international sanctions had deprived the Russian state of at least $450 billion in war funds since February 2022, including through lost oil tax revenues and immobilized central-bank reserves.
While Trump’s waiver leaves existing sanctions in place, it blocks the additional pressure the new measures were intended to exert.
Trump has also pushed Ukraine to scale back its own campaign against Russia’s energy sector, repeatedly urging Kyiv to halt strikes on Russian fuel infrastructure and suggesting that those attacks are driving up U.S. fuel prices. But Ukrainian strikes are only one factor affecting fuel prices; disruptions linked to the war with Iran are largely responsible for constraining global supplies, while reduced Russian refinery activity has added pressure to diesel markets.
On Oct. 11, Trump claimed that Russia and Ukraine had agreed to an “ energy ceasefire .” But Ukrainian officials reported Russian strikes on energy infrastructure hours after his announcement, and Zelensky said the purported agreement was news to him.
That same day, Trump called for Ukraine to replace President Volodymyr Zelensky . Withdrawing sanctions within a week of those statements, and thereby improving Russia’s financial position,  points to an increasingly uneven display of support from the U.S.
Jeff Colgan, a professor of political science at Brown University, tells TIME that the decision is basically “is like trading [Ukrainian] democracy for diesel.”
A diesel deal with Russia, announced Oct. 9, is one of the many recent actions Trump has taken in an effort to reduce fuel prices for Americans ahead of midterm elections on Nov. 3. The deal came with an easing of sanctions as the Treasury Department issued a temporary license authorizing transactions involving Russian diesel, including imports into the U.S., through April 7, 2027.
In his announcement of the deal on Truth Social, Trump said that Russia had agreed to supply global markets with more than 4.8 million tons of diesel, claiming  that it would bring diesel prices down “IN RECORD NUMBERS.”
But experts question whether the deal would actually have any material impact on the price of fuel for American consumers.
“There could be a modest short-term effect on wholesale prices, but only a small and delayed benefit, if any, would reach consumers at the pump,” says Tatiana Mitrova, a fellow at Columbia University&#x27;s Center on Global Energy Policy.
The waiver signed Friday, in comparison, could potentially prevent additional disruption to the global energy market . It allows Russian oil to continue flowing to importers like China and India, preventing further market volatility.
“The broader sanctions waiver may prevent additional market disruptions, but it does not itself create new supply,” Mitrova says. “The potential economic benefit to American consumers looks rather modest compared with the geopolitical cost of weakening sanctions pressure.”
Reducing that pressure—which has widely been interpreted as a component of the diesel deal, though it has not been explicitly identified as such—likely benefits Russia more than the monetary value of the diesel deal itself, Mitrova points out.
“The much bigger financial benefit for Moscow is avoiding additional sanctions that could have undermined its much larger crude oil export revenues,” she says.
And while Trump has touted the benefits of the deal, Colgan questions whether they will be tangible to any country other than Russia itself.
“Easing the sanctions probably won&#x27;t move global prices very much (big market), but could mean substantial extra revenue for Russia,” he says.
But the potential consequences extend beyond Russia’s balance sheet. The waiver also raises questions about Washington’s commitment to Ukraine as relations between the two countries deteriorate.
Beyond its impact on global diesel prices, Trump’s sanctions waiver could deepen a growing rift between the U.S. and Ukraine—raising further questions about Washington’s support for Kyiv.
Konstantin Sonin, a Russian economist and a professor at the University of Chicago Harris School of Public Policy, reads the move as a symbolic gesture of support for Russian President Vladimir Putin. He suggests that it could send a message to U.S. allies that extends beyond economic considerations.
“I think this will be read by Ukraine and Ukraine leadership—and European leadership—as another step in the United States withdrawing from being an active European player,” he tells TIME.
In addition to bolstering Russia’s finances and giving it latitude in its invasion of Ukraine,  Sonin says that the waiver of additional sanctions could also “boost morale” for Putin.
“It’s another bit of signaling that the United States is not as supportive of Ukraine as they were historically,” he adds. “You still cannot say that the United States is on Putin&#x27;s side, but it is another bit of diminishing support for Ukraine.”
Mitrova agrees that the biggest benefit to Russia might be more strategic than financial.
“For Putin, the most valuable outcome may not be additional diesel revenues but the perception that Washington is increasingly prioritizing domestic energy prices over sustained pressure on Russia,” she says. “That could reinforce the Kremlin’s belief that it can outlast Western support rather than make meaningful concessions.”
Zelensky criticized the decision to block the new sanctions.
“The decision to undo all of this, to block all of it, seems wrong to me,” the Ukrainian President wrote on X on Saturday. “And it definitely helps only the Russians. It only fuels their satisfaction and their desire to keep killing people. This certainly does not stop the war.”
Mitrova argues that in easing sanctions, the U.S. also compromises its position with European leadership that has continued to align closely with Zelensky and Ukraine.
“Europe is still tightening sanctions against Russia, while Washington is now moving in the opposite direction,” she says. “By suspending sanctions that were designed to discourage major countries from buying Russian energy, Washington risks undermining a central pillar of the Western strategy toward Russia.”
Why Republicans and Democrats are criticizing the waiver
Trump’s waiver earned a flood of criticism from both sides of the aisle, particularly since it undermined a widely supported act of Congress. The Sanctioning Russia Act of 2026 cleared Congress on Sept. 16 with overwhelming bipartisan support: a vote of 86-11 in the Senate and a vote of 262-159 in the House.
North Carolina Republican Sen. Thom Tillis, who is leaving office in January, called the decision “beyond irresponsible” on Saturday.
Suggesting that the Trump Administration is overtly snubbing the will of the legislative branch, Tillis wrote on X : “It’s unconscionable that this administration is capitulating to a Russian dictator who illegally invaded Ukraine.”
Texas Republican Sen. John Cornyn reacted by saying “Lindsey would be appalled,” referencing the late Republican Sen. Lindsey Graham—a staunch Ukraine supporter after whom the sanctions act was named. Graham had spent over a year petitioning for the move before his death on July 11.
Massachusetts Sen. Elizabeth Warren, a Democrat, said in a statement that Trump was “enabling Vladimir Putin to continue his war of aggression.”
Senate Democratic Leader Chuck Schumer released a statement Saturday saying that “by giving the Russian economy life support, Trump’s deal with the devil keeps getting dirtier.”
Trump was able to waive the sanctions through a safety valve provision embedded in the legislation. The law allowed Trump to waive its sanctions and tariffs after submitting a written certification to Congress that doing so was in the national interest, along with a report explaining that determination. In Friday’s order, Trump said he had submitted both.
“I am hereby waiving the application of such sanctions, restrictions, and duties provided for in the Graham Act,” Trump wrote in the order. “I have submitted to the Congress a certification that the issuance of such a waiver at this time is in the national interests of the United States.”
Trump has not yet publicly disclosed how the move served national interests, and the White House did not reply to TIME’s request for comment.
Darline Graham, the late senator’s sister who was appointed to fill his seat in South Carolina and is running for election this November to keep it, said at a campaign stop on Saturday that the decision was Trump’s to make; however, she voiced disapproval, saying that she would prefer to “keep the pressure on Russia.”
“I don’t trust Putin and I don’t trust Russia,” she said.

Another interest rate hike may be ahead as Fed battles inflation
Fed Chair Kevin Warsh says the AI investment boom is contributing to inflation, even as he predicts the technology will bring huge economic benefits.
Fed Chair Kevin Warsh says the AI investment boom is contributing to inflation, even as he predicts the technology will bring huge economic benefits.
Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh said Friday he won’t prejudge what the U.S. central bank will do about interest rates at its upcoming meetings in October and December.
The Fed increased its key interest rate by a quarter-point to about 3.9% in September, its first increase in three years, and stubbornly elevated U.S. inflation will likely lead it to hike that rate again in coming months to slow spending and help cool price pressures.
But Warsh offered no fresh signals during a “fireside chat” with International Monetary Fund Managing Director Kristalina Georgieva at a meeting of financial leaders in Bangkok.
RELATED STORY | Another interest rate hike could be coming before the end of year
“At the Federal Reserve, we made very clear that we would deliver price stability, and we removed a dose of accommodation about a month ago,” Warsh said. “I don’t prejudge meetings. I don’t believe in saying now what I might do at the meetings that we have in the balance of this year."
The Fed's September rate hike defied U.S. President Donald Trump’s repeated calls for the Fed to cut rates. The president criticized the Fed’s rate-setting committee, though he still expressed support for Warsh, whom he appointed earlier this year.
The global surge in prices is a key concern of central bankers and financial leaders meeting in Bangkok.
According to minutes released from the most recent meeting, most Fed officials expect that another interest rate increase will likely be needed this year. That would raise borrowing costs for mortgages, car loans, and credit cards over time.
Warsh said the surge in investment driven by the craze for artificial intelligence is one of the factors behind higher inflation and the Fed's task force on issues related to AI will meet Nov. 16.
“I’d say the advent of this new technology is probably the most consequential change to the U.S. and the global economy in my lifetime to this point,” he said.
Unlike military conflict and other shocks that are driving prices higher, Warsh said he believes AI will bring “huge benefit” to the world economy in time.
For Fed economists who struggle to foresee the future using data that lags behind today's realities, AI could prove a vital tool, he said.
RELATED STORY | Higher interest rates are dragging down consumer stocks
"We torture data with old fashioned models to try to find out what’s underlying inflation trends. Not so long from now, it won’t be torture. We will have access at the fingertips of central banks to all the information in the world, and we’ll be able to use new analytical tools to distill that data,” he said.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Donald Trump’s decision to pause sanctions against Russia for its war against Ukraine has been criticised by Republicans and Democrats.
Donald Trump’s decision to pause sanctions against Russia for its war against Ukraine has been criticised by Republicans and Democrats.
Ukraine war briefing: Trump’s sanctions waiver is a ‘60-day pause’ to analyse economic impact, says Rubio
Ukraine’s Zelenskyy expects Trump to lift more sanctions against Russia. What we know on day 1,698
US secretary of state Marco Rubio has said that US President Donald Trump’s waiver of sweeping sanctions and tariffs on Russia would be a 60-day pause to “analyse” their impact on the global economy. Rubio said also no one at the White House ordered the slow down of the speed of Ukraine’s access to Starlink to prevent it from being used on drones flying faster than 100mph, referring to Starshield, the military version of Elon Musk’s Starlink satellite internet network.
“The president wants to take the 60-day period of time to sort of analyse what imposing both sanctions and tariffs will mean to the global economy and even to the US economy,” Rubio said, noting that the economic restrictions involved a number of countries, not just Russia. Pointing to high energy prices and the Iran war, “we need that period of time to sort of study all this and figure out what the right combination is,” he said.
Trump’s abrupt decision to waive sanctions on Russia days before they were to take effect drew outrage Saturday from members of Congress , which had overwhelmingly passed the legislation. Republican Senator Rick Scott said it was clear Russia was the aggressor, and said the US had “to do everything we can to put economic pressure on Russia”. On social media, fellow Republican Thom Tillis described the move as “beyond irresponsible” and a capitulation “to a Russian dictator who illegally invaded Ukraine”.
The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy expects the Trump administration to lift more sanctions against Russia in the future and has called for Europe to establish its own negotiations with Moscow as the US has sought better ties and business deals with the Kremlin. “Unfortunately, the lifting of sanctions will continue, and I believe that the ‘diesel deal’ is the first step,” said Zelenskyy on Saturday. “Unfortunately, it seems to me that this cannot stop Putin; on the contrary, he feels joy and happiness that he has such support.”
Zelenskyy said Ukraine’s lead negotiator, Rustem Umerov, had met Friday with US presidential envoy Jared Kushner, Trump’s son-in-law . Zelenskyy said the US was working to arrange a three-way meeting involving him, Trump and Putin, potentially in the Middle East, Europe or the US.
Russian air defence ⁠units intercepted and destroyed nearly ⁠200 Ukrainian ⁠drones ​headed for Moscow on Saturday, ⁠Mayor Sergei Sobyanin said on Telegram. Sobyanin, ⁠in a long ​series ‌of ‌online posts, noted ‌that 195 drones had been destroyed since midnight, with emergency crews examining debris on ‌the ground. Russia’s civil aviation authority, ​Rosaviatsia, said two Moscow airports – Domodedovo and Vnukovo – had ⁠suspended flight operations.
Romania said Saturday it shot down two Russian drones over the Black Sea, the latest in a spate of incidents believed to be spillover from Russia’s invasion of Ukraine . Ships have been attacked in recent days by drones in the Black Sea off the coast of Romania and Bulgaria.

Tsvetana Paraskova is an energy and commodities journalist who has contributed to Oilprice.com for nearly a decade, covering global energy markets, commodities,&#8230;
Middle East Chemicals Keep Their Cost Edge Despite Hormuz Crisis
Middle Eastern petrochemical producers retain a major cost advantage, despite the Iran war disrupting exports and forcing buyers to diversify supplies.
U.S. chemical companies are benefiting from the crisis, with plastics prices surging over 50% and Dow reporting a 27% jump in quarterly plastics segment sales.
- The Gulf's dominance is unlikely to fade, as abundant low-cost feedstocks and massive production capacity keep buyers dependent on the region.
Despite the Iran war and the Hormuz crisis, the Middle East’s low-cost chemicals production is set to retain its long-term advantage over other regions.
Near-term shifts in the sourcing of polymers are taking place due to the current constraints in the Persian Gulf’s industrial base, benefiting U.S. chemicals companies.
But in the long run, the Middle East is likely to retain its buyers’ base because it still has the two levers to be competitive on the global market—an extensive production base and, most of all, low-cost production with abundant naphtha supply, industry analysts and executives tell Reuters .
Many buyers have moved to diversify their polymer supply due to the Middle East crisis and the fewer volumes they have received through the Strait of Hormuz since the Iran war began at the end of February.
But many customers see the diversification as protection against the current crisis, not a replacement in the long run, as the low prices of the Middle East’s polymers trump risks to supply for most.
Thus, the cost advantage of the Middle East remains a key driver despite the Hormuz crisis. Related: Global Gas Crunch Makes Europe’s Remaining Coal Plants Profitable
Buyers continue to base their purchasing decisions on price, Morningstar analyst Christian Flemming told Reuters.
Even as supply disruptions have prompted diversification, the current bottlenecks in the Middle East are unlikely to reduce the long-term importance and advantage of the Gulf producers in the polymer markets, a spokesperson for chemicals giant BASF told the publication.
The Middle East is the world’s biggest exporter of polyethylene, the most used plastic globally.
Exports of polyethylene from the Middle East accounted for 43% of all global exports of the polymer last year, data from the International Trader Publications showed earlier this year. The Middle East exported 18.7 million tons out of total global exports of 43.8 million tons in 2025.
North America was the second-largest polyethylene exporter last year with 15.6 million tons. Because of secure feedstocks and ample supply, North America is set to fill some of the supply gap amid the Hormuz crisis, according to International Trader Publications.
Exports of polymers of propylene (or polypropylene) from the Middle East have been the least affected by the war among 12 key commodities and materials, falling by 24% between April 2025 and April 2026, the UN said in an August analysis .
This drop compares with a 54% decline in combined export volumes, a massive 95% slump in LNG exports, an 83% plunge in urea shipments, an 80% slump in methanol, and a 75% crash in ammonia exports from the Middle East.
But overall, as a result of the war, the prices of polyethylene and polypropylene, the world’s second-most widely used commodity plastic after polyethylene, have jumped by more than 50% since March.
Cheap and abundant feedstock amid soaring plastics prices have made U.S. chemicals producers the immediate winners in the global competition as the Iran war is constraining raw materials and capacity in other regions that depend on Middle Eastern supply.
Higher-cost Asian petrochemicals manufacturers early this year announced reductions in capacity runs as their naphtha supply was either too expensive or trapped at the Strait of Hormuz. High energy costs continue to pressure the European chemicals industry, which has been rationalizing and idling capacity since the 2022 energy crisis.
U.S. giant Dow, for example, reported net sales in the Packaging & Specialty Plastics segment of $6.4 billion for the second quarter, up by 27% from a year earlier.
“This was primarily driven by higher polyethylene pricing, which lifted local price by more than 40%,” Dow’s chief financial officer Jeff Tate said on the Q2 earnings call in July.

### 10. ★★★☆☆ Ukraine War, Day 1,697: Trump Camp Proposes Renewed Europe Purchases of Russia's Gas - EA WorldView

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-18T06:07:12+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Ukraine War, Day 1,697: Trump Camp Proposes Renewed Europe Purchases of Russia's Gas&nbsp;&nbsp;EA WorldView

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Ukraine War, Day 1,697: Trump Camp Proposes Renewed Europe Purchases of Russia's Gas
Vladimir Putin greets Donald Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner (L) and real estate developer Steve Witkoff (C) in the Kremlin, Moscow, Russia, September 5, 2026
EA on International Media: Selling Out Ukraine — Trump Buys Russia’s Diesel Fuel and Cuts Off Intelligence
EA-Frontline VideoCast: Allying with Putin, Trump Betrays Ukraine
Ukraine War, Saturday's Coverage: Trump Refuses To Implement New Russia Sanctions
In a further sign of its evolving support for the Kremlin, the Trump camp is proposing that Europe renew its purchases of Russia's gas.
The European Union, whose members have been criticized for years for maintaining imports despite Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, plans to cut off all purchases of oil and gas in January.
Donald Trump had demanded that Europeans restrict the imports. However, his envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner brought up the renewal in talks since September, including with Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelensky in Kyiv.
The envoys proposed that Ukraine restart pipelines that used to carry Russian natural gas to Europe. European officials in the talks have recoiled at the idea.
In the past nine days, Donald Trump has agreed to purchases of Russian diesel fuel, undermining US sanctions, and refused to implement the Graham Act for tougher measures against Moscow and the top five countries purchasing its oil and gas.
Early-warning intelligence to Ukraine, affecting both frontline operations and air defenses, was halted for two consecutive nights amid Russian missile and drone attacks. Starshield, the military version of Starlink, was restricted.
The Trump camp are also proposing that Europe transfer to it $1 billion in frozen Russian assets. European leaders have rejected the step.
The developments are all part of a reported Trump effort to offer economic incentives to Russia to re-enter talks to end its invasion. The Kremlin has spurred the effort by offering deals, worth tens of billions of dollars, benefitting allies of Trump, Witkoff, and Kushner.
Vladimir Putin's financial advisor Kirill Dmitriev, the chief manipulator of Witkoff and Kushner since spring 2025, said at an energy conference on Friday, “I think we will be announcing some important initiatives in the near future. The ice has finally broken, after all.”
Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelensky, harshly criticizing the Trump overtures to the Kremlin, predicted in an interview this week, “The question will be when will the next deal be? Maybe it will be gas. Definitely it will be gas.”
Vladimir Putin has reportedly proposed to Witkoff and Kushner that the US join Moscow in renewal of Nord Stream gas pipelines from Germany to Russia, damaged by attacks in September 2022.
Ukraine stopped transporting Russian gas in January 2025, giving up around $1 billion in annual transit fees. Last year, 13% of the EU's gas imports came from Russia, compared to 45% in 2021.
US Secretary of State Marco Rubio played down the Trumpist move towards the Kremlin in comments to journalists on Saturday.
Rubio said of the purchase of Russian diesel, "I think the President wants to take the 60-day period of time to sort of analyze what imposing both sanctions and tariffs will mean to the global economy and even to the US economy."
Rubio insisted that the Trump camp was not responsible for the restriction on Starshield earlier this week: "No one at the White House ordered anyone to slow down internet or slow down speed so that certain drones couldn’t use it."
Two US officials and a Western official have said that the order to limit Starshield came from "inside the White House".

Share US and Iran both claim control of Strait of Hormuz amid attacks on vessels on social media
A vessel transits the Gulf of Oman off the coast of Khor Fakkan in the emirate of Sharjah, eastern UAE, on October 13, 2026 [AFP]
Tehran, Iran –  Dozens of ships were attacked in or near the Strait of Hormuz in October as Iran and the United States compete to assert their authority over the waterway.
At least 12 ships were attacked in the week ending on October 16, the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) reported late Friday, with one important caveat.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 Fighting in Yemen intensifies: What’s the latest on the battlefield?
list 2 of 3 Iraq devalues dinar to ease cash crunch, leaving traders in limbo
list 3 of 3 Containers and debris captured floating around the Strait of Hormuz
“UKMTO is struggling to accurately report true number of attacks with majority of vessels attacked refusing to report to UKMTO,” the maritime security organisation stated. It said it was aware of more incidents in the waterway via other sources but could not verify them due to a lack of information.
The same pattern has been true for the first half of October. The month has seen the highest number of ship attacks since the start of the US-Israel war on Iran, which began on February 28.
Of the 12 vessels that were confirmed attacked, the organisation said half continued to their destination or reached port. One sank, two were disabled, and three were either damaged or had an unconfirmed outcome.
The UKMTO also reported a new type of attack on Saturday. The master of a tanker said their vessel was hit by a rocket-propelled grenade (RPG) fired from a small boat 78 nautical miles (equivalent to about 144km) northeast of Saudi Arabia’s city of Jubail, Eastern province, resulting in damage to the aft port quarter.
In recent months, the US has managed to increase energy transits through the Strait of Hormuz and surrounding waterways through a shuttle system coordinated with Arab powers . But volumes have oscillated weekly due to rising Iranian attacks.
Oil flows through Hormuz were at 11.6 million barrels per day (bpd) over the seven days to October 13. This was about 68 percent of the 17.1 million bpd pre-war baseline, according to Kpler figures.
On Friday, Brent crude also touched $110 a barrel for the first time since May in light of ongoing attacks. The situation has been exacerbated by the Iran-aligned Houthis’ attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure and their capture of the Bab al-Mandeb strait. Expectations that the conflict between the US and Iran was escalating have also impacted prices.
The US military has provided a starkly different outlook on the situation in and around the Strait of Hormuz, which accounted for roughly a fifth of global oil and natural gas flows before the war began.
US Central Command (CENTCOM) chief Brad Cooper claimed in a video message late Friday that despite the Iranian attacks, 400 ships and 100 million barrels of oil moved through the Strait of Hormuz over the past week.
“Let me be clear: The Strait of Hormuz is controlled by the United States and our regional partners, not Iran,” he said, attributing the greatly increased ship attacks to “desperation” by the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC).
The US military also claimed that it continues to prevent Iranian exports and imports via its southern ports through an “ironclad” naval blockade . It claims the blockade has cost Iran $25bn in oil export income, and that the US has recently seized $350m in Iranian cryptocurrency financing.
The UKMTO report said total outbound transit over the last seven days was 125 ships, with total inbound transits standing at 129. Over the week, most transits by the Western security agency were for dry bulk cargo, followed by tanker products and general cargo.
Tehran has not provided exact figures on how many vessels have been attacked, or how many are passing through the Hormuz, although it has said Washington is exaggerating numbers to project success of its naval campaign.
It comes as two vessels were reportedly hit by naval mines in the Strait of Hormuz this week, with the US and Iran offering contradictory claims about the situation.
The IRGC has increasingly reported that vessels are hitting mines across the strategic waterway, which it says remains unsafe for vessels which do not coordinate with Iran’s Persian Gulf Strait Authority, an entity set up during the war to manage passage through the Hormuz.
The latest claim came on Saturday, when the IRGC said a supertanker carrying more than two million barrels of crude oil caught fire after striking a mine while moving through an “unauthorised” route.
The Panama-flagged container shipping vessel Ten Neptune transits the Gulf of Oman off the coast of Khor Fakkan in the emirate of Sharjah, eastern UAE, on October 13, 2026 [AFP] Iran’s state-run Press TV shared footage it says showed the tanker on fire. But CENTCOM said it had cleared the strait of naval mines, and that vessels are being struck by projectiles.
The latest incident “fact-checked” by the US military was on Thursday, when it said a civilian container ship, the M/V GFS Pride, was hit by an IRGC projectile, not by a mine as the Iranians had claimed. The vessel sank as it was being towed to shore.
The US has refrained from responding to any of the recent vessel attacks, but there are concerns that Washington could escalate the war after the November 3 midterm elections.
Diplomacy has not fully halted, with messages continuing to be relayed between the two parties by regional mediators like Pakistan, Qatar and others. But no breakthrough in talks is expected any time soon.
President Donald Trump has reportedly ordered the US military to remain prepared for launching bigger attacks after the elections. Those attacks could potentially include using the GBU-43/B Massive Ordnance Air Blast bomb, nicknamed the “mother of all bombs” , according to US media.
But Tehran has signalled that it intends to continue the attacks, with acting Defence Minister Majid Ibn Reza saying on Saturday that it will be Iranian missiles, not US bombs, that determine the future of the war.
“Your bombs will explode elsewhere; in your global reputation, the US economy, and even your electoral calculations,” he was quoted as saying by state media.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Fed holds interest rates steady in 1st move since Iran war spiked oil prices
Oil prices have surged in recent weeks, threatening wider inflation.
The Federal Reserve held interest rates steady on Wednesday at its first meeting since the U.S.-Israeli war with Iran drove up gasoline prices and risked a wider bout of inflation.
The central bank&#x27;s move marked the second consecutive time it has opted to maintain interest rates at current levels since the outset of 2026. Before that, the Fed cut interest rates a quarter-point three straight times. The decision on Wednesday matched market expectations.
"The implications of developments in the Middle East for the U.S. economy are uncertain," the Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC), a policymaking body at the Fed, said in a statement on Wednesday.
The rate decision appeared to worsen an already-woeful trading day on Wall Street. The Dow Jones Industrial Average closed down 768 points, or 1.6%, while the S & P 500 dropped 1.3%. The tech-heavy Nasdaq declined 1.4%.
The FOMC issued a forecast projecting a one quarter-point interest rate cut over the remainder of 2026 and another quarter-point cut in 2027.
The 12-member board voted 11-1 to maintain the current level of interest rates. Stephen Miran, a former Trump administration official who recently joined the board, cast the lone dissenting vote. He called for a rate cut.
Elevated price increases have coincided with a slowdown of economic growth, threatening to intensify an economic double-whammy known as " stagflation ," which poses difficulty for the Fed.
Can the US petroleum reserve stop the rise in oil prices? Experts weigh in
Inflation stood at 2.8% in February, according to the Personal Consumption Expenditures index, the gauge preferred by the Fed. Price increases register nearly a percentage point higher than the Fed&#x27;s target rate of 2%.
"These elevated readings largely reflect inflation in the goods sector, which has been boosted by the effects of tariffs," Federal Reserve Chair Jerome Powell said at a press conference in Washington, D.C., on Wednesday.
In this Jan. 13, 2026, file photo, the Federal Reserve Board Building is shown as it undergoes renovations, in Washington, D.C.
"Near-term measures of inflation expectations have risen in recent weeks, likely reflecting the substantial rise in oil prices caused by the supply disruptions in the Middle East," Powell added.
If the Fed opts to lower borrowing costs, it could spur growth but risk higher inflation. On the other hand, the choice to raise interest rates may slow price increases but raises the likelihood of a cooldown in economic performance.
Bend, Oregon, shooting: Suspect ID&#x27;d, had attended elementary school, police say
Iran live updates: Trump admin prepares options, including &#x27;mother of all bombs&#x27;
Hurricane Isaias: At least 4 dead as Isaias downgraded
The benchmark rate stands at a level between 3.5% and 3.75%. That figure marks a significant drop from a recent peak attained in 2023, but borrowing costs remain well above a 0% rate established at the outset of the COVID-19 pandemic.
A lackluster jobs report last week showed the U.S. economy lost 92,000 jobs in February, which marked a reversal of fortunes for the labor market and erased most of the job gains recorded in 2026.
The unemployment rate ticked up from 4.3% in January to 4.4% in February, the BLS said. Unemployment remains low by historical standards.
A revised government report last week on gross domestic product (GDP) showed the economy grew at a sluggish annualized pace of 0.7% over the final three months of 2025.
Those economic headwinds helped set the conditions before the outbreak of war with Iran, which spiked oil prices and risked price increases for a host of diesel-fuel transported goods.
U.S. crude oil prices rose to about $97 per barrel on Wednesday, marking a surge of more than 50% since a month earlier.
Since the military conflict began, U.S. gas prices have gone up 86 cents to an average of $3.84 per gallon as of Wednesday, according to AAA .
Will the rise in oil prices drive up inflation? Experts weigh in
The rate decision on Wednesday marked the first such move since a federal judge blocked Justice Department subpoenas to the Federal Reserve&#x27;s Board of Governors after determining the government "produced essentially zero evidence" to support a criminal investigation of Fed Chair Jerome Powell, according to an unsealed court opinion.
"A mountain of evidence suggests that the Government served these subpoenas on the Board to pressure its Chair into voting for lower interest rates or resigning," U.S. District Judge James Boasberg said in his opinion on Friday.
Acting U.S. Attorney Jeanine Pirro blasted Boasberg as an "activist" judge and pledged to appeal his ruling.
ABC News&#x27; Alexander Mallin, Allison Pecorin, and Jack Date contributed to this report.

### 15. ★★★☆☆ Trump eases Russia oil sanctions, but is Europe still buying Moscow’s gas? - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T11:16:54+00:00
- **发生了什么**: <a href="https://news.google.com/rss/articles/CBMiswFBVV95cUxOb2J3andQTW1UOC1yU3dPTUdSZHB2R0l4SVlwSFB6VzN2a2VoSnc3SWhjcnF3cTd0RG5KcVB3UldJOW5FU29TWkg2TkM1bFNqc0F1dXF6WFl1Y2ItbU84UlpYMTNVQ3RxNkNGVE9oNURSREtKNXp5UU15U1BKOFB4dllmNDlpLXdIM0lucWoyTlFBWTNfZXlZenRtQ0M1YzlUOHg3U0VsWC1WSHVBNHFiRFhZTdIBuAFBVV95cUxQa1NLdm9ma2pEWjVNczBpY3VfeFpHcjVQelNreTE0QjV2eEQ0RGlzcmxkSU1ndkVOSi1LMnl6cS1EU19hZmE1dGJRT3ZCZDN2VDdSOUNyWEZWRG9ZemtRalVmNXV2SHFqc0pvVHBQdFRoT3VQM0EtWEotWG9pRVJfNE5rREhlanRQbzB3Vl9VM3llZGljN3JyXz

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Trump eases Russia oil sanctions, but is Europe still buying Moscow’s gas? on social media
Photo provided by the French Army shows a French army NH90 helicopter over the oil tanker Tagor, which is under international sanctions and was travelling from Russia in the Atlantic Sea, on Sunday, May 31, 2026 [File: French Army via AP]
United States President Donald Trump has signed an executive order waiving newly-passed Russia sanctions, claiming the move is in the US’s “national interest”.
Earlier this month, Trump also announced a deal to bring stocks of Russian diesel into global markets, in a bid to drive down energy prices that have spiked during the US-Israel war on Iran. The US Treasury waived sanctions on Russian diesel sales until April 2027.
Trump’s embrace of Russian energy has angered Ukraine, European partners and many US officials, who say the move rewards Moscow while it continues its four-and-a-half-year war against its neighbour.
Ukraine’s President Volodmyr Zelenskyy slammed Trump’s diesel deal as “a gift” to Russia’s President Vladimir Putin that will help fund his war effort.
While European countries have also criticised Trump’s embrace of Russian energy, the US president has accused them of “hypocrisy”, as figures show that European Union countries continue to import Russian gas.
In his executive order on Friday, Trump called off numerous planned Russia-related sanctions days before they were due to take effect, including duties on Russian goods and penalties for buying the country’s oil and gas.
The now-blocked measures were part of legislation passed in Congress with bipartisan support last month , named after the late US Senator Lindsay Graham, an outspoken critic of Russia.
They would have included up to 100 percent tariffs on all goods brought into the US from the five top importers of Russian crude oil or gas, including China and India. They could also have imposed equal penalties on countries deemed to have knowingly bought oil or gas from Russia 30 days after the law’s implementation, or to be among the top five countries helping Moscow circumvent sanctions on sales of its oil.
The waiver will hold until the Trump administration believes “further action is appropriate”, according to the executive order.
US Congressman Don Beyer, a Democrat from Virginia, said the sanctions waiver amounts to a “surrender to Putin” that “betrays our Ukrainian partners and defies clear congressional intent”.
Vasily Astrov, an economist at the Vienna Institute for International Economic Studies, told Al Jazeera that the Russian government will likely be the greatest beneficiary because the easing of sanctions means Russian oil will not have to be further discounted.
He estimated that about 60 cents of every extra dollar in the price of a barrel of Russian oil would “end up in state coffers”.
Russian official Kirill Dmitriev, an envoy for Putin, hailed the US decision to waive the sanctions as “an important and truly historic moment”.
Other beneficiaries include major importers of Russian oil and gas, such as China and India, who are now less exposed to the risk of secondary US penalties.
While Trump hopes the measures will also help bring down oil prices for US consumers before November mid-term elections, experts say that is less certain.
Addressing the US’s recent diesel deal with Russia, EU economy chief Valdis Dombrovskis said it is unlikely to have a significant impact on global prices.
According to Trump, that deal would bring 300,000 tonnes of diesel fuel into US and global markets “immediately”, followed by 500,000 tonnes in November, and 1 million tonnes afterwards.
“If we look at the volumes, they are not of any macroeconomic significance, and frankly, there are strong doubts whether Russia is even able to supply those volumes, and according also to US quality standards, those are big question marks,” Dombrovskis told the AFP news agency.
China and India are the largest buyers of Russian crude oil, purchasing 6 billion euros ($6.74bn) and 3.6 billion euros ($4.04bn) worth respectively in September, according to the Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air (CREA), a nonprofit think tank.
Hungary and Slovakia, the landlocked EU members that have exemptions to the bloc’s ban on Russian crude, also remain big customers, buying a combined 290 million euros ($326m) worth of the commodity in September, said CREA. Turkiye imported 172 million euros ($193m) worth of Russian crude oil the same month, the think tank added.
Since the end of 2022, the largest importers of Russian oil products, which include diesel, have been Turkiye, China, Saudi Arabia, Brazil and Singapore.
Saudi Arabia imported the most Russian oil products in September at 534 million euros ($600m), followed by Turkiye with 321 million euros ($361m), China with 304 million euros ($342m) and India with 161 million euros ($181m).
Is Europe buying increasing amounts of gas from Russia?
“The European Council regrets the United States’ decision to temporarily lift sanctions on Russian diesel,” European leaders said in a joint statement adopted following a two-day European Council meeting in Brussels this week.
But despite its own near-blanket ban on Russian oil, the EU has continued to import large quantities of Russian gas, though it plans to phase out those imports next year .
Urgewald, a Germany-based group that researches coal companies, reported this week that more than 12 million tonnes of Russian Arctic LNG was transported into EU ports from January to September this year, a 9.5 percent jump from the same period last year.
In September, EU countries imported 817 million euros ($918m) of pipeline gas, and 615 million euros ($691m) of liquefied natural gas (LNG), according to CREA.
EU members France and the Netherlands were the largest LNG importers, bringing in 446 million euros ($501m) and 116 million euros ($130m) respectively, according to the think tank. France’s LNG imports represented a 103 percent increase from the previous month, it added.
France’s President Emmanuel Macron has been among the most outspoken about Trump’s diesel deal with Russia, saying on Friday that it “sends a negative message” and would have no impact on global prices.
Hungary imported the most Russian pipeline gas of any EU member by far during the month, with 414 million euros ($465m) worth. Slovakia and Bulgaria brought in 161 million euros ($181m) and 132 million euros ($148m), respectively, according to CREA data.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

CENTCOM says 'vast majority' of Iran's attempted attacks on Strait of Hormuz are 'unsuccessful'
U.S. Central Command spokesperson and U.S. Navy Capt. Tim Hawkins speaks to Fox News Digital about Operation Epic Fury against Iran.
The "vast majority" of Iran's attempted attacks on shipping in the Strait of Hormuz are "unsuccessful" as the key waterway remains open for transit, U.S. Central Command spokesperson and U.S. Navy Capt. Tim Hawkins told Fox News Digital this week.
Hawkins spoke after CENTCOM said the GFS Pride, a Singapore-flagged, Cyprus-owned container ship was hit by an Iranian projectile in the Strait of Hormuz Wednesday and sunk while being towed ashore due to a fire on board the ship.
"The vast majority of Iran's attempted attacks on innocent civilian mariners transiting an international waterway, being the Strait of Hormuz, they are unsuccessful," Hawkins said.
"As a matter of fact, the U.S. military has assisted in providing coordinated protection for thousands of ships that have safely transited.
LIVE UPDATES: IRANIAN ARMY SPOKESPERSON SAYS STRAIT OF HORMUZ WILL REOPEN 'IF AMERICA LEAVES THE REGION'
"That translates to about 97% of the ships that have sought to transit the Strait of Hormuz have done so successfully without being hit by an Iranian projectile.
"But let's be clear here. What the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps of Iran is doing is unjustified, and it's also unsuccessful because they are not achieving their objective of closing the Strait of Hormuz," he added.
"It is open, it is available for transit and we've seen that transit continue this very day."
An MH-60R Sea Hawk helicopter lands on the flight deck of the USS Rafael Peralta as it transits regional waters, in this photo released by U.S. Central Command Oct. 15, 2026.  (CENTCOM)
In an update Friday from CENTCOM Commander Adm. Brad Cooper, he said, "In recent days and months, we have seen the IRGC continually attack merchant ships transiting the Strait of Hormuz, and, in many cases, kill innocent civilian mariners.
"The International Maritime Organization reports that more than 20 civilian mariners have been killed by Iran's attacks against commercial shipping since February," Cooper continued.
"Over the last seven days alone, about 400 merchant ships have sailed through the Strait. U.S. forces have also assisted Gulf partners in shipping over 100 million barrels of crude oil during the same period, adding to the more than 1.3 billion barrels of crude we have helped regional allies ship through the Strait in recent months."
Hawkins also told Fox News Digital he thinks "every American should be proud of American service members who are serving in the Middle East, in particular focusing on doing two things at once as it relates to Iran.
VIDEO: ISRAEL'S AMBASSADOR TO THE UN ISSUES STARK WARNING TO IRAN, HEZBOLLAH DURING ELECTION PERIOD
Vessels in the Strait of Hormuz near Musandam, Oman, Oct. 2, 2026. (Reuters)
"And one is helping maintain flow through the Strait of Hormuz in the form of commercial vessels despite Iran's unjustified aggression," Hawkins said, noting that thousands of ships have crossed through the waterway over the last few months, "in addition to more than 1 billion barrels of crude oil.
"At the same time that they are encouraging traffic flow in support of international shipping, they are maintaining a steel wall blockade against Iran. There have been no exports or imports to Iran without U.S. permission in the form of allowing humanitarian ships to go through," Hawkins continued.
"So, that shows the precision, dedication and professionalism of our troops who are executing this complex mission — not just one — but two at the same time."
More than 50,000 U.S. service members are stationed across the Middle East, according to Hawkins.
A U.S. Air Force F-15E Strike Eagle is refueled by a KC-135 Stratotanker while patrolling the skies over the Middle East, according to CENTCOM, in this image released on Oct. 2, 2026. (CENTCOM)
"They are highly vigilant, they are highly dedicated and they're serving with courage and bravery. And they're doing so alongside our regional allies and partners. It shouldn't be forgotten that we continue to stand shoulder to shoulder with our Gulf allies and partners to help them defend their airspace from unjustified and unwarranted attacks from Iran, and we have been highly successful in working together. And will continue to do that," he said.

Apple Retail Staff Told to Warn Against iPhone Duo Walk-In Purchases
Saturday October 17, 2026 2:30 pm PDT by Hartley Charlton
Some Apple retail employees have been told to warn customers against visiting to purchase an iPhone Duo without a pre-order on Friday, October 23.
Apple Store employees told MacRumors that they have been advised that there will likely be no &zwnj;iPhone Duo&zwnj; stock available for walk-in purchases on launch day. Staff have been instructed to discourage customers without pre-orders from coming in expecting to buy the device, warning that they will be disappointed.
Apple's online store tells customers to check back on Saturday, October 24 for pickup availability. Pickup reservations were briefly available when pre-orders opened before the option was disabled.
The October 24 message does not guarantee stock will be available that day, and it remains unclear how firm the warning shared with employees is and whether it applies worldwide. If the guidance is firm and widely perpetuated, it closes off a potential route for customers hoping to bypass the device's lengthy online wait times, at least at the stores concerned.
Warnings about a constrained launch began well before Apple announced the &zwnj;iPhone Duo&zwnj;. In early September, Nikkei Asia reported that production had reached only a few hundred devices per day in late August. Further production problems emerged in late September.
Earlier this month, Bloomberg 's Mark Gurman said Apple had at one point believed production constraints could push the start of sales into November. The company subsequently assembled enough inventory to proceed with the October 23 launch, although supply remained very limited .
The extent of the shortage became apparent when pre-orders opened earlier this week. Delivery estimates moved beyond launch day within minutes, and within 30 minutes, all configurations in Apple's U.S. online store showed January 25 shipping estimates. The device is now effectively sold out for Christmas through Apple's online store.
Apple subsequently described demand as " nothing short of extraordinary " in a statement to the Financial Times , adding that it was working to get devices to customers as quickly as possible. Apple has not publicly specified whether any stores will have stock for launch-day walk-in purchases.
The &zwnj;iPhone Duo&zwnj; starts at $1,999 in the United States, with the first pre-order deliveries scheduled for October 23.
Apple Exec Says 'Hold Us Accountable' on iPhone Duo Crease
Friday September 18, 2026 9:51 pm PDT by Juli Clover
Apple hardware engineering VP Tom Marieb spoke with TechRadar and shared some candid thoughts about Apple's upcoming iPhone Duo.
The iPhone Duo uses a matte nano-texture display, which is meant to cut down on glare and minimize the visibility of the crease. When asked if the iPhone Duo's crease would be more visible over time, as that's what tends to happen on other foldables, Marieb said...
Thursday October 1, 2026 1:37 pm PDT by Joe Rossignol
Apple on Instagram today shared a curated gallery of six photos shot on the iPhone Duo. The photos were shot by American photographers Jake Michaels and Jason Nocito, as part of a project commissioned by Apple.
iPhone Duo is equipped with four cameras:A rear 48-megapixel Fusion Main camera

Makes more sense to me business wise if i can meet all the demands of my costumers and they are satsified with my product. Means, they had a good experence with buying stuff from me, they are going to remember that, and come see me next time they need to purchase another phone or something else.

There's a contingent of people whose goal is &#34;complain about Apple&#34;. I don't buy the idea that Apple was limiting supplies to create scarcity.
1. On October 12, four days before preorders opened, 9to5Mac reported that Apple had increased its iPhone Duo supply-chain orders by approximately 30%, according to China's Securities Times. The revised target was reportedly approaching 10 million units for the remainder of 2026. Increasing orders by 30% (assuming the report is accurate) is hardly what we'd expect from a company whose principal objective was to create an artificial shortage.
2. Long ship times have a cost. Customers may postpone purchases, buy competing products, or decide they don't want the Duo after all. If the backlog extends beyond the holiday season, Apple also risks losing sales it could otherwise have captured immediately. In sum, a lack of availability turns excitement about the Duo into disappointment. Apple is much better served by having a lot of these Duos in the hands of customers.
3. The purpose of creating deliberate scarcity is to generate hype and desirability. Apple already had plenty of it associated with this launch. It had no need to damage sales and anger its customers just to create more.

### 22. ★★★☆☆ UAE agrees with Saudi Arabia to re-enter Yemen conflict amid Houthi gains

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T11:57:15+00:00
- **发生了什么**: United Arab Emirates will reportedly resume payments to Yemeni forces as gains by Iran-backed Houthi fighters alarm Gulf state rulersYemen civil war: what sparked latest escalation and who is involved?The United Arab Emirates has agreed to re-enter the Yemen conflict by resuming paym

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Fighters loyal to the Houthis take part in a rally to recruit more fighters in Sana’a. Photograph: Mohammed Huwais/AFP/Getty Images
Fighters loyal to the Houthis take part in a rally to recruit more fighters in Sana’a. Photograph: Mohammed Huwais/AFP/Getty Images
UAE agrees with Saudi Arabia to re-enter Yemen conflict amid Houthi gains
United Arab Emirates will reportedly resume payments to Yemeni forces as gains by Iran-backed Houthi fighters alarm Gulf state rulers
Yemen civil war: what sparked latest escalation and who is involved?
The United Arab Emirates has agreed to re-enter the Yemen conflict by resuming payments to some government forces fighting Iran-aligned Houthis in the south, according to sources in Yemen. In return, Saudi Arabia has agreed to provide air cover for the anti-Houthi force.
The new cooperation agreement is the first tangible sign that the deep rift between the UAE and Saudi Arabia over Yemen’s future is starting to be healed – if only because the advance by Houthi forces across Yemen has alarmed all the rival Gulf state rulers.
Saudi Arabia is facing daily drone and missile attacks and is struggling to maintain an atmosphere of normality, as Houthi strikes immobilise its airports and some of its energy infrastructure.
Southern forces – mainly in the Giant Brigades – were previously trained and funded by the UAE, and are regarded as some of the most effective in the disjointed anti-Houthi military coalition. But the UAE quit operations inside Yemen in January, cutting off its funding supply line.
Neither Saudi Arabia nor the UAE have responded to a demand this week by southern Yemen separatists for a referendum on secession, a move that would restore the 1967-1990 borders, when Yemen was two separate states.
Yemeni Southern Giants Brigades fighters celebrate and wave their weapons. Photograph: Yemen Giants Brigades Media Centre/AFP/Getty Images The separatist Southern Transition Council has also not yet agreed for its troops to fight outside the borders of the old Southern Yemen, but an explicit agreement by Saudi Arabia to allow a referendum would be likely to lead to the lifting of that restriction.
Yemen’s UN-recognised government, based in Aden, declared last week a counteroffensive to drive Houthi forces from the capital Sana’a, but so far the military drive has met with limited success around Majzar near Marib and the Bab al-Mandab strait.
There is also no direct contact between the STC leader, Aidarous al-Zubaidi, and Saudi officials. Zubaidi fled to Abu Dhabi in January after a failed STC attempt to take charge across the south and announce a referendum on separation in two years’ time. In response, the Saudis bombed UAE arms shipments for STC forces.
The UAE then announced it was quitting all involvement in Yemen , and the Saudi-backed government said it would seek to try Zubaidi for treason.
Food and fuel run low as besieged Yemeni city braces for Houthi breakthrough
UAE officials are deeply concerned by the state of the conflict. Despite massive losses, the Houthis are determined eventually to capture Taiz and Marib, the two remaining cities outside the boundaries of the old Southern Yemen.
On Friday two missiles fired by the Houthis struck the airport of Aden while a passenger plane was preparing to take off. No casualties were reported.
The missiles landed about 500 metres from the Yemenia Airways aircraft, and approximately 100 metres from the midpoint of the runway, said Yemen’s information minister, Moammar Al-Eryani.
The strike came a day after at least five people were killed and 16 injured in a Houthi attack on a presidential palace in Aden.
Egypt’s president, Abdel Fatah al-Sisi, said he was convening an emergency session of parliament to grant his country’s military the powers to take action in defence of Saudi Arabia. He stressed Egypt would not be taking any offensive action, and would only defend Saudi Arabia.
In the UK, discussions are continuing about a Saudi request for further military aid. The UK has sent an RAF Voyager air-to-air refuelling aircraft, but regional diplomats are pressing for the UK to do more. Some concern is being expressed over whether Riyadh should be engaging more closely with other Gulf States, including Oman and Qatar, on the issue.
The legal implications of a blanket UK backing for the largely Saudi air campaign is also being considered, since the court of appeal in 2019 found the UK had acted unlawfully in issuing arms export licences during a previous Saudi bombing campaign of Yemen in 2015-16. The Campaign Against Arms Trade said the UK had made no proper assessment of potential Saudi breaches of international humanitarian law, forcing the UK to revise its procedures.
Saudi Arabia claims to have revised its methods substantially since then, and certainly argues that it is acting in self-defence in line with the UN charter. The UN security council on 15 October adopted a UN presidential statement on 15 October asserting Saudi Arabia’s right to act in self-defence in line with international law.
No accurate death toll in the war since August is available, but more than 200,000 people have been displaced, according to the UN, which is placing increasing pressure on Aden-based humanitarian groups.

### 23. ★★★☆☆ ‘I cannot carry all of this alone’: the couples kept apart by the war in Gaza

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T07:00:24+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Families have been scattered, fathers separated from their children, and couples unable to reunite and marryWijdan Abu Matar, 43, was forced to leave Gaza in November 2023 with her eight children for medical treatment, while her injured husband, Hatem, remained behind.Wijdan and her family were living in the Nuseirat refugee camp when the Gaza war began with the 7 October 2023 Hamas attack on Israel. Ten days later, on 17 October that year, an Israeli strike hit the al-Muhandsee

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Wijdan Abu Matar, front, has managed to bring her children together in Cairo, Egypt, but her husband, Hatem, remains in Gaza. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian
Wijdan Abu Matar, front, has managed to bring her children together in Cairo, Egypt, but her husband, Hatem, remains in Gaza. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian
‘I cannot carry all of this alone’: the couples kept apart by the war in Gaza
Families have been scattered, fathers separated from their children, and couples unable to reunite and marry
Wijdan Abu Matar, 43, was forced to leave Gaza in November 2023 with her eight children for medical treatment, while her injured husband, Hatem, remained behind.
Wijdan and her family were living in the Nuseirat refugee camp when the Gaza war began with the 7 October 2023 Hamas attack on Israel. Ten days later, on 17 October that year, an Israeli strike hit the al-Muhandseen tower, near their home, injuring Wijdan and every member of her family.
“We were sitting together, just about to start lunch when the bombing happened,” Wijdan recalled. “The ceiling collapsed over our heads. Everything went dark. I couldn’t see anything. I just started screaming the names of my sons and daughters.”
Her daughter Nour, 15, suffered the most serious injuries, including a fractured skull and bleeding in the brain. Five-year-old Amna suffered severe burns to her chest. The family was initially separated after the strike.
Nour spent about 33 days in intensive care, with Wijdan by her side. Amna and Aysel, three and a half, were at a different hospital for 15 days.
Aysel, who incurred severe burns in the bombing, holds up a photograph of her father. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian On 30 November 2023, Wijdan travelled with Nour to a hospital in Egypt for treatment. For months, the family remained scattered across Egypt, receiving medical treatment and staying with other relatives.
“I felt completely helpless,” Wijdan said. “There was a fire in my heart because I was away from my husband and my family was scattered.”
Eventually, Wijdan managed to bring her children together in an apartment in Egypt. Three years on, however, she is still separated from her husband, who remains in Gaza. “I have become both the mother and the father to my children,” she said. “The responsibility is exhausting me, and I am constantly worried about my husband.”
Hatem suffered a serious leg injury that limits his mobility and is now displaced in a tent in Nuseirat. Even speaking to him is difficult because of Gaza’s unreliable internet connection. “When he manages to get online, he calls me and the children. He tells me, ‘I am counting the days until I can see you again.’ I can see the tears in his eyes,” Wijdan says.
Speaking to Hatem is difficult because of Gaza’s unreliable internet connection, Wijdan says. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian Wijdan has no regular source of income and relies on donations. Hatem stopped receiving a salary 10 months ago and his injury prevents him from working.
In Egypt, life is difficult for Wijdan and her family. She says she cannot afford transportation to send five of her children to school. “I see my children’s future slipping away in front of me, and there is nothing I can do.”
Nour still requires medical treatment, but Wijdan often cannot afford her medication. She sometimes considers returning to Gaza to be with Hatem, but fears exposing her children to further suffering.
“I dream of getting my children the treatment they need, and I hope we can reunite with my husband as soon as possible so he can also receive treatment,” Wijdan said. “I cannot carry all of this alone. Psychologically, I am exhausted. I have lost 35 members of my family in this war.”
‘Psychologically, I am exhausted. I have lost 35 members of my family in this war,’ says Wijdan Abu Matar. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian Since the war began with Hamas’s attack on Israel that killed 1,200 people, at least 73,783 Palestinians have been killed and 174,738 wounded, according to the health ministry. Earlier this year an independent UN inquiry found that Israel continues to commit genocide by deliberately targeting Palestinian children in Gaza.
‘All I want to do is cry’: trauma of 7 October survivors three years after the attacks
Sami Abu Tabaq, a 29-year-old Palestinian photojournalist, was forced to leave the Gaza Strip in April 2024, leaving behind his family and his fiancee, Ola.
“Loneliness kills me every day,” he said. “Life away from the woman I love and my family is incredibly hard. I’m desperately waiting for the day God brings us together again.”
Sami became engaged to Ola five months before the war began. They had met by chance when he chose her family’s home as a location for a photoshoot. Before the war, they saw each other constantly. They went to the sea, had dinner at restaurants in Gaza, and spent months preparing the house where they planned to live after their wedding, which was scheduled for November 2023.
Sami and his family fled their home in Tal al-Zaatar, Jabaliya, early on in the war. Their home was badly damaged and later destroyed, along with the house Sami and Ola had been planning to move into once they got married.
When his sister decided to flee south with her children, Sami accompanied her but he could not reach Ola to say goodbye.
The separation became unbearable. Days, sometimes weeks, passed without Sami knowing whether Ola was alive. Once, he saw reports that an entire residential block near her home in Gaza City had been bombed. “I almost lost my mind. I had never felt so helpless,” he said. Unable to return north, he asked his brother to check on her. Eventually, his brother confirmed that she and her family were alive.
‘I couldn’t cry over my children like everyone else’: the tragedy of Palestinian journalist Wael al-Dahdouh
Sami continued working as a photojournalist from southern Gaza. Four journalists who had shared his tent were later killed, including Hamza al-Dahdouh, a son of the Al Jazeera journalist Wael al-Dahdouh.
As Israel threatened a ground invasion of Rafah in 2024, Ola began urging Sami to leave Gaza, fearing he would be killed. Sami paid a company coordinating exits at the time for both of them to cross into Egypt. But Ola could not reach southern Gaza to travel through Rafah. “She told me, ‘You go, and I’ll follow you,’” Sami said. “And here we are, still waiting, nearly two and a half years later.” They still have not managed to get married.
By his second day in Egypt, Sami was overwhelmed by guilt. “The overthinking kills me,” he said. “I felt ashamed to turn on the lights or eat proper food while my fiancee and my family were struggling to find the most basic things.”
In Egypt, his life has become dominated by loneliness and sleeplessness. “There is safety in Egypt and there are beautiful places, but I stay home. Happiness has no meaning without her. I cannot enjoy anything while she is not here,” he said.
Sami Abu Tabaq sits alone in his apartment in Cairo, while his fiancee, Ola, remains in Gaza. Images of Gaza are shown on the television. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian Despite the war, Ola completed her bachelor’s degree in nursing at the Islamic University of Gaza. She has also endured enormous loss: her cousin, the journalist Ayat Khadoura , was killed, along with about 50 other members of her extended family.
From Egypt, Sami tries to create moments of happiness for her. For her latest birthday, he contacted her brother in Gaza and secretly arranged a cake, gift and flowers. “When she woke up and saw everything, she was so happy. I love her reaction when she’s happy.” The only problem was the flowers. “They were artificial,” he said. “I wasn’t happy with them, but natural flowers can’t get into Gaza because Israel does not allow them in.”
Sami says he has tried every possible way to get Ola out, without success. She is now applying for scholarships abroad, hoping education might provide a route to evacuation and, eventually, to him.
Sami recently travelled to Mecca for Umrah, where one prayer remained constantly on his mind. “I prayed, ‘God, please bring us together soon,’” he said. “But one thing I know for sure: I will wait for her for the rest of my life, until God brings us together.”

### 26. ★★★☆☆ Berkshire shares buck market sell-off, rally to highest since Buffett said he would step down as CEO

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T12:46:21+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Berkshire Hathaway shares are at their highest level since Warren Buffett told shareholders at the May 2025 annual meeting he planned to step down as CEO at the end of that year.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Berkshire rally to highest since Buffett announcement
(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)
Berkshire Hathaway shares closed Friday at their highest level since Warren Buffett told shareholders at the May 2025 annual meeting he planned to step down as CEO at the end of that year .
The day before that Saturday surprise, both the  Class A and Class B shares had closed at all-time highs.
Over the next three months, they lost more than 14% of their value as investors worried a " Buffett premium " was dissolving.
It&#x27;s been more than a year since their post-meeting low in August of 2025, and the recovery has not been a straight line, but BRK.A ended Friday at $798,000, just 1.4% below its all-time closing high of $809,350.
The B shares finished at $532.02, also 1.4% under their all-time closing high of $539.80.
A loss Thursday broke a 10-session winning streak that added 5% to Berkshire&#x27;s stock prices but they rebounded with gains of more than 1% Friday.
CNBC Pro thinks investors may be happy Berkshire is spending some of its big cash pile under CEO Greg Abel, with  $4.5 billion of buybacks in Q2 , investments in  Alphabet , Taylor Morrison Home , Lennar , (although there haven&#x27;t been any buys of that stock since October 2), and now, Bending Spoons . (More on that below).
It also notes Keefe, Bruyette & Woods analyst Meyer Shields believes higher short-term interest rates are helping to lift the shares.
Berkshire still has roughly $360 billion in cash, "a good symbol of continued patience under the new CEO," and those higher rates mean "they&#x27;ll be making more money" on that money.
In addition, its insurance operations have benefited from a quiet hurricane season so far.
Higher oil prices are good for its $19 billion stake in  Chevron , which closed Friday at an all-time high after gaining almost 36% since the start of July. Berkshire&#x27;s energy-related  subsidiaries are also getting a tailwind.
And recently, Berkshire may be getting a boost as investors  shift away from high-flying AI and tech stocks and turn to "old" economy names .
For example, Coca-Cola , a long-time Buffett favorite and now a $37.6 billion holding , also closed Friday at an all-time high, perhaps with some extra help from KFC&#x27;s switch from Pepsi to Coke . It&#x27;s up 34% year-to-date.
Berkshire&#x27;s position has gained $2.4 billion since last Friday, overtaking Alphabet to become the third largest stake in its portfolio. (See the full top 10 below.)
Berkshire&#x27;s B shares have cut their June 1 deficit of 17.5 percentage points versus the  S&P 500 by almost two-thirds.
They are now 6.0 percentage points behind the benchmark for the year.
Atypical bet on &#x27;buzzy&#x27; Italian tech company is big win ... so far
Berkshire Hathaway&#x27;s surprising bet of close to $1 billion on  Bending Spoons is paying off big, at least in the short term.
The Italian company, which began trading on Nasdaq in the U.S. in July, buys cast-off internet names like AOL, Evernote, and Vimeo, uses AI to cut costs, and deploys the savings to make more acquisitions.
In late July, CNBC Pro said , "Wall Street is buzzing," and "analysts ... see big gains ahead."
The tech stock is decidedly not a typical Berkshire  value play , trading at a large multiple to its earnings.
Matteo Danieli, co-founder of Bending Spoons, center, during the company&#x27;s initial public offering (IPO) at the Nasdaq MarketSite in New York, US, on Wednesday, July 1, 2026.
But before Wall Street&#x27;s open Wednesday morning, a Bending Spoons  news release revealed Berkshire is investing a "further" $330 million in the company by purchasing 10 million newly issued shares at $33 per share in a private placement .
That was already an almost 12% discount from Tuesday&#x27;s closing price of $37.86 and the news sent Bending Spoons shares even higher .
They hit an intraday high of $58.56 before closing up 41% at $53.46 on Wednesday.
Bending Spoons shares are up 24.3% over the three trading days since the revelation, after giving up a chunk of their earlier gains in  Friday&#x27;s tech sell-off to end at $47.07.
That&#x27;s a solid 42.6% higher than Berkshire&#x27;s $33 purchase price for a profit, on paper, of nearly $141 million.
But what&#x27;s that "further" in Bending Spoon&#x27;s statement?
The release also disclosed, apparently for the first time, that Berkshire invested $600 million at Bending Spoon&#x27;s IPO more than three months ago.
The release doesn&#x27;t specify how many shares Berkshire bought in July, and Bending Spoons wouldn&#x27;t confirm my math, but assuming Berkshire paid the $29 per share IPO price, that initial purchase would have been almost 20.7 million shares.
That puts Berkshire&#x27;s holding now at close to 30.7 million shares, which would be worth more than $1.4 billion at Friday&#x27;s close, a gain of 55.3% for its overall investment of $930 million.
Bending Spoons shares soar after Berkshire reveals $330M investment, taking a 4% stake
Bending Spoon&#x27;s release also says the "new investment brings Berkshire Hathaway&#x27;s ownership to 4%."
The company told me in an email it hasn&#x27;t "specifically disclosed" how many outstanding shares it has now, "but we had 635 million fully diluted weighted average shares outstanding in Q2, we issued 34 million shares as part of the IPO, and we will be issuing an additional 10 million as part of this transaction."
Using those numbers, Berkshire&#x27;s presumed 30.7 million shares are a 4.5% stake, and that&#x27;s what I&#x27;ve put in the  Berkshire Portfolio Tracker for now.
We may learn more next month about exactly how many shares Berkshire bought in the IPO when its portfolio snapshot as of September 30 is due to be disclosed.
Another unknown: who at Berkshire made the call to take a stake in Bending Spoons.
Bending Spoons CEO Luca Ferrari: We&#x27;ll buy any company as long as we think we can make it better
Bending Spoons CEO and Co-Founder Luca Ferrari shares Wall Street&#x27;s enthusiasm about Berkshire&#x27;s investments, saying in the news release the Omaha company "has been among the finest and most respected capital allocators in the world, and is a longstanding inspiration for Bending Spoons."
Morningstar&#x27;s Greggory Warren (who was one of the designated analyst questioners at Berkshire shareholders meetings from 2014 to 2019 ) writes the Bending Spoons buy, along with Berkshire&#x27;s now $36 billion stake in  Alphabet , show "the firm is finally moving out of its comfort zone to find places to put capital to work, which should be viewed as a positive for long-term investors."
At Barron&#x27;s , Andrew Bary says , "Many Berkshire holders would like to see the company invest more heavily in stocks given the company&#x27;s $350 billion-plus in cash, but Bending Spoons was a surprise" given its high valuation.
Bloomberg on Yahoo Finance: JPMorgan&#x27;s Combs Directs $5 Billion in Spending Since Joining
The Motley Fool on Yahoo Finance: Berkshire Hathaway Outperformed the S&P 500 Over a 60-Year Period Under Warren Buffett. Here&#x27;s the Stock That Could Help Continue the Streak Under Greg Abel.
Warren Buffett defends Coca-Cola , a big and long-time Berkshire Hathaway holding, against health concerns over sugary beverages.
WARREN BUFFETT : Yeah well — people have been drinking Coca-Cola since 1886, so I imagine that there&#x27;s been some —
I don&#x27;t know of any study that&#x27;s been made on the lifetime of heavy users of Coca-Cola versus people who drink nothing but water.
But speaking for myself, I&#x27;m 84, literally one-quarter of all the calories I&#x27;ve consumed in the last 30 years have been Coca-Cola. And I don&#x27;t see how anybody can feel any better than I feel.
I am one-quarter Coca-Cola, and I can tell you, I haven&#x27;t missed a day of work, you know — since I had the prostate cancer — radiation — three years ago. And I don&#x27;t think the prostate cancer was caused (laughs) by Coca-Cola.
So there&#x27;s 1.9 billion eight-ounce servings of Coca-Cola products sold around the world today, and there will be more than 1.9 billion a year from now, and more than that five years from now, and more than that ten years from now.
And that&#x27;s been true.  And some people will like other things, and — and if they like broccoli and Brussels sprouts, God bless them, you know, but don&#x27;t invite me for dinner.  (Laughter)
