### 28. ★★★☆☆ US, Israel face threats of war if oil prices keep rising - The Jerusalem Post

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T19:27:05+00:00
- **发生了什么**: US, Israel face threats of war if oil prices keep rising&nbsp;&nbsp;The Jerusalem Post

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Oil prices over $100 could affect war with Iran, leading to escalation before elections - analysis
The US and Israel know that the main hurdle to keeping the lid on the war with Iran is the price of oil, and it will keep rising if no solution can be found for Hormuz and the Houthis.
Vessels transit the Strait of Hormuz off the coast of the port city of Bandar Abbas in southern Iran on September 5, 2026; illustrative
( photo credit : ATTA KENARE / AFP via Getty Images )
High oil prices are going to be an issue over the next few weeks if they continue to stay above $100 a barrel. That is a clear challenge for the United States and also potentially for Israel in terms of keeping a lid on a renewed conflict with Iran prior to the elections in both countries.
It is no secret that the looming elections have shifted dynamics, expectations, and assessments of whether the conflict could restart.
The high oil prices come amid a decline in stock prices on Wall Street, amid concerns about AI companies and energy prices. That could tend to be a kind of triple threat affecting the United States, and thus also affecting Israel.
That is because the United States has been working hard to keep diesel and oil prices from skyrocketing. There has been a lot of messaging that the Strait of Hormuz is open and that other energy sources exist, but the question is whether the global market will accept those assertions.
Recent videos showing ships sinking in the Strait of Hormuz and all sorts of containers floating away as ships are in distress are certainly not something that will boost global optimism regarding supply chains, and it always remains a question whether enough ships are transporting energy supplies.
Flames burn at a facility belonging to Saudi oil giant Aramco south of Riyadh on October 3, 2026. (credit: AFP VIA GETTY IMAGES)
It also leads to questions about what the Houthis might do. The Houthis could try to threaten the Bab el-Mandeb Strait. They could also encourage other Iranian proxies, such as those in Iraq, to continue attacks on Saudi oil and gas energy infrastructure.
A mysterious explosion in Iraq on Saturday appears to have struck an Iranian proxy group there. All this could continue to disrupt global trade. For example, when attacks hit pipelines or transit points in Saudi Arabia, repairs can take weeks. That creates disruption, and all those disruptions add up to the feeling in the global market that there is too much uncertainty.
This kind of uncertainty will affect the US. It already appears to be affecting policy regarding the Ukraine-Russia war, and this is the kind of thing that can lead to escalation in the Middle East or miscalculations in which something happens again with Iran.
Meanwhile, Iran continues to be blockaded , but it also tends to feel a bit confident about its next steps. As a result, we see potential for escalation within the next week or two.
Iran also knows there are elections in Israel and the United States; therefore, Iran and its proxies may want to take advantage of this period.
The one email that makes sense of the Middle East. Iran, the Gulf, Syria, Turkey, and whatever moved that day, from The Jerusalem Post, every evening.

3 U.S. Energy Stocks Investors Are Watching As Oil And Gas Prices Stay High
Inflation is heating back up, fuel and wholesale costs are biting, and policy risks around war, tariffs and the midterms are reshaping where money can quietly work for or against you. That mix is punishing some corners of the market while lifting others tied to elevated oil and gas prices. This article examines three US energy stocks related to those developments and outlines key points to consider as you make portfolio decisions.
The three stocks covered below are just a starter pack, and the full screen surfaced 25 more U.S. energy producers and infrastructure companies with equally compelling stories that are not included here. To go deeper, analyze and identify your own highest-conviction ideas directly through the U.S. Energy Producers and Infrastructure Benefiting from Elevated Oil & Gas Prices screener .
Overview: Natural Gas Services Group rents and services natural gas and electric compression equipment that keeps U.S. oil and gas production facilities flowing.
Operations: Natural Gas Services Group generates about US$182 million from compression rentals, with smaller contributions from sales and aftermarket services, entirely in the United States.
Natural Gas Services Group sits right in the flow of elevated U.S. gas production, supplying the compression units producers rely on when volumes stay high and infrastructure runs hard. That is exactly where this screener is focused.
"The accelerating growth in global LNG exports and increasing demand from sectors like AI data centers and power generation are driving higher natural gas production, which directly benefits Natural Gas Services Group through strong contracted rental growth and high utilization of compression assets and can support future revenues and EBITDA."
What ultimately happens to rental margins if one persistent cost pressure continues to build faster than customers accept higher pricing?
If that pressure is what you are weighing, read the full narrative for Natural Gas Services Group to see how contracting, balance sheet strength, and growth optionality could offset it.
NYSE:NGS Revenue & Expenses Breakdown as at Oct 2026 Viper Energy (VNOM)
Overview: Viper Energy owns mineral and royalty interests across the Permian Basin, collecting oil and gas income when operators drill and produce.
Operations: Viper Energy generated about US$1.9b from acquiring oil and natural gas properties, with all activity tied to assets in the United States.
Viper Energy appears in this screener because its revenue is closely tied to oil and gas prices, collecting a share of production revenue without funding drilling budgets or field operating crews.
"Viper avoids drilling capital, but it also gives up drilling control. Operators decide when to develop the acreage."
What ultimately happens to cash generation, dividends, and growth will depend on how assumptions about future operator activity develop over time.
That trade off is exactly what the full narrative for Viper Energy unpacks, showing where Viper Energy's royalty model could accelerate, stall, or quietly decouple from headline oil moves.
NasdaqGS:VNOM Earnings & Revenue Growth as at Oct 2026 California Resources (CRC)
Overview: California Resources produces oil, gas and power in California and runs carbon capture and storage projects through its Carbon TerraVault platform.
Operations: California Resources reports about US$3.4b from Oil and Natural Gas, with total US revenue of roughly US$3.7b.
California Resources matters in this screener because it is tightly tied to California fuel pricing, local energy security and inflation driven oil and gas moves, giving the business direct exposure to the same forces pushing fuel costs higher for consumers.
"Recent and potential regulatory reforms in California aimed at improving oil and gas permitting and supporting local energy security may provide California Resources with increased operational flexibility and access to its extensive project inventory, which could support higher production levels and revenue growth."
The key issue is how one unresolved pressure on California Resources' cost structure ultimately affects margins if pricing power weakens.
If that cost squeeze is what you are weighing, read the full narrative for California Resources to see how California Resources could turn regulatory shifts into accelerating cash power.

National Gas Prices Stay Near Recent Highs as Crude Oil Nears $100
Washington, D.C. &#8211; The national average for a gallon of regular gasoline is down a couple of cents from last week at $4.33. Gas prices aren’t budging much as crude oil prices remain high, once again nearing $100.00 per barrel.
The national average has stayed above $4.00 per gallon since late July and is currently more than a dollar higher than it was this time last year. As the graph below shows, the fall months typically bring lower gas prices, but volatility in the oil market has led to seasonal record highs this year.
According to new data from the Energy Information Administration (EIA), gasoline demand decreased last week from 8.76 million barrels per day to 8.40 million. Total domestic gasoline supply increased from 204.7 million barrels to 208.3 million. Gasoline production decreased from 9.34 million barrels per day to 9.23 million barrels.
At the close of Wednesday’s formal trading session, WTI rose 95 cents to settle at $95.09 per barrel. The EIA reports that U.S. crude oil inventories decreased 8.6 million barrels to 415.6 million barrels, 3% below the five-year average.
The national average per kilowatt hour of electricity at a public EV charging station stayed the same this past week at 41 cents.
The nation’s top 10 most expensive gasoline markets are California ($6.23), Hawaii ($5.63), Washington ($5.42), Nevada ($5.42), Oregon ($4.97), Alaska ($4.96), Idaho ($4.85), Utah ($4.82), Illinois ($4.69), and Arizona ($4.66).
The nation’s top 10 least expensive gasoline markets are North Carolina ($3.70), Georgia ($3.77), Texas ($3.83), Tennessee ($3.88), Indiana ($3.89), Louisiana ($3.90), South Carolina ($3.90), Mississippi ($3.91), Arkansas ($3.93), and Alabama ($3.94).
The nation’s top 10 most expensive states for public charging per kilowatt hour are Hawaii (54 cents), West Virginia (52 cents), Louisiana (49 cents), California (47 cents), Alaska (46 cents), New Hampshire (46 cents), Arkansas (44 cents), North Dakota (44 cents), Arizona (44 cents), and Nevada (43 cents).
The nation’s top 10 least expensive states for public charging per kilowatt hour are Kansas (32 cents), Missouri (32 cents), Iowa (33 cents), Nebraska (34 cents), Maryland (35 cents), South Dakota (35 cents), Utah (35 cents), Vermont (36 cents), Minnesota (37 cents), and New Mexico (37 cents).
Drivers can find current gas and electric charging prices along their route using the AAA TripTik Travel planner .

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Former Michigan gubernatorial candidate Tudor Dixon discusses Vice President JD Vance's visit to Michigan, focusing on affordability and the crucial midterm elections. Dixon highlights the GOP's confidence in key races and criticizes Democrats for opposing DHS funding. The visit follows a West Bloomfield synagogue attack, raising concerns about terror links to Hezbollah within the state.
Vice President JD Vance faced questions from Michigan voters during a town hall in Flat Rock as the state emerges as a key battleground in the fight for Senate control in November's midterms.
The town hall hosted by CBS News came just weeks before the midterm elections as Republicans are fighting to keep their majorities in the House and Senate. Vying for the open Senate seat in Michigan is Democratic candidate Abdul El-Sayed and Republican candidate Mike Rogers, a former member of the House of Representatives.
Vance places blame on Biden administration for high gas prices
The first question Vance fielded Saturday came from a 43-year-old Detroit barber, who asked him why gas prices remain significantly higher than during Trump's first term.
Vance first pointed to Iran’s attacks on commercial shipping and the resulting disruption to global energy markets, but he also placed blame on the Biden administration.
"That's actually the biggest reason why gas prices are so high, because when Joe Biden was president, we had about 20% of American refining capacity that went offline," Vance said.
Vance said the Trump administration was using the Defense Production Act to help restore domestic refining capacity, but cautioned that rebuilding that infrastructure would take time.
CBS News anchor Tony Dokoupil pushed back on Vance's attempts to blame Biden, noting that Trump and Vance have been in office for nearly two years while Americans continued to face high gas prices.
MORNING GLORY: PRESIDENT TRUMP AND THE AMERICAN MILITARY HAVE BEATEN IRAN
US Vice President JD Vance speaks at a MAGA Inc. super PAC event in Sterling Heights, Michigan, August 31, 2026. Saul Loeb/Pool via REUTERS
Vance pressed on White House corruption allegations
Dokoupil shifted the conversation to government corruption, citing a Fox News poll showing that 60% of voters were extremely concerned about the issue. He asked Vance whether anything he had witnessed inside the Trump White House made him feel "squeamish."
"You certainly see evidence of corruption in Washington, D.C. Do I see evidence of corruption from the president of the United States, from the administration? No, Tony, I just don’t," Vance said.
The exchange came days after Kimberly Guilfoyle resigned as U.S. ambassador to Greece amid allegations that she sought $100,000 from a donor seeking access to Trump administration officials. Guilfoyle has denied wrongdoing.
Dokoupil pressed Vance on the allegations involving Guilfoyle, along with concerns about restrictions on IRS scrutiny of Trump's tax returns and the president's cryptocurrency earnings.
Vance said selling access to the White House would be "disgusting and despicable" and that anyone responsible should face consequences.
Dokoupil then asked whether Vance would comply with subpoenas investigating alleged corruption if Democrats took control of Congress in January.
"Of course, of course I will," Vance said. "Look, we follow the law. I follow the law."
KIMBERLY GUILFOYLE FACES POTENTIAL SENATE PROBE OVER BOMBSHELL $100K ALLEGATION
President Donald Trump hired U.S. Ambassador to Greece Kimberly Guilfoyle after her engagement to his eldest son fell through and now, after Donald Trump Jr. has married, his administration might be forced to fire her. (Bloomberg via Getty Images)
Vance acknowledges 'imperfect' immigration rhetoric
Vance was confronted over his immigration rhetoric by a naturalized U.S. citizen who said she voted for the vice president but questioned why the administration was "scapegoating immigrants."
The voter, who immigrated to the United States 20 years ago, challenged Vance over his characterization of certain H-1B visa workers as "foreign indentured servants."
"To the extent that my rhetoric has been imperfect here, that's on me," Vance said.
But Vance defended his remarks, arguing that his criticism was aimed at companies exploiting foreign workers rather than immigrants themselves. He said the H-1B visa system gives employers too much power over foreign workers whose ability to remain in the country depends on their employment, while also allowing companies to replace American workers with visa holders.
"That's not a criticism of the H-1B visa holder. That's a criticism of a company who brings a person in, who sometimes will literally lay off an American worker," Vance said.
Dokoupil pressed Vance on whether his rhetoric and that of the Trump administration dehumanized immigrants.
"My goal here is not to dehumanize any immigrant. And in fact, I love a number of immigrants," Vance said.
But Vance argued that allowing too many immigrants into the country too quickly could undermine social cohesion and fundamentally change American communities.
"I have neighbors, I have friends of mine who literally don't know a single person in their neighborhood who speaks the language they do," Vance said. "I don't think it's unreasonable for an American citizen to want to share their community with people they have something in common with."
Though the town hall was pre-recorded, Vance took to X shortly after it aired to share footage from showing a burning building in France amid mass riots. He said what's unfolding in France is a cautionary tale for the United States.
"Western liberals imported the third world into France and other countries in the name of diversity and compassion. They ignored fundamental questions of assimilation and compatibility," Vance posted on X.
"They were told it would lead to this and they didn't listen. I pray that France, a beautiful country of amazing people, will stop this madness before more of its landmarks burn," Vance continued.
A boat floats as commercial vessels appear anchored in the Strait of Hormuz off Bandar Abbas, Iran, Tuesday, Oct. 13, 2026. (Razieh Poudat/ISNA via AP)
Vance claims U.S. has destroyed Iran's nuclear program
Dokoupil pressed Vance on why the war with Iran had dragged on longer than the Trump administration anticipated, asking the vice president, "Why haven't we won decisively?"
Vance argued that the U.S. had "in some ways" already won the war, claiming that American military strikes had successfully destroyed Iran's nuclear program, which he described as the central reason Trump launched the operation.
"Have we destroyed the Iranian nuclear program? The answer is yes," Vance said.
But Vance said the administration was still working to secure a "long-term" and "verifiable" commitment from Iran that it would not rebuild its nuclear capabilities, while also preventing Tehran from disrupting global energy supplies.
Vance said the Iranians responded by terrorizing commercial shipping through the Strait of Hormuz , disrupting global oil supplies and driving up gas prices.
He claimed gas prices would be "three times as high" if it weren't for the U.S. Navy protecting commercial shipping through the waterway.
VANCE TAKES AIM AT SOFT-ON-CRIME DEMS AS GOP FACES UPHILL MIDTERM BATTLE: ‘LOST THEIR MINDS’
Vance blasts El-Sayed over synagogue attack response
Vance was also urged by a Michigan voter to condemn antisemitism within his own political base, including rhetoric from prominent conservative figures such as Tucker Carlson.
The question came from Eddie Rubin, a lifelong Democrat whose 5-year-old daughter and 3-year-old son survived the attack at Temple Israel, which occurred in March in Michigan.
The attacker, a Lebanese-born U.S. citizen, rammed his pickup truck through the synagogue's entrance before exchanging gunfire with security. The attacker was killed, but no children or synagogue staff members were injured.
"I condemn anti-Semitism. I condemn Jew hatred," Vance said.
But Vance also turned the conversation to Michigan's Senate race, criticizing Democratic candidate Abdul El-Sayed for his response to the synagogue attack. Vance pointed to El-Sayed's remark that "hurt people hurt people."
"He went around and said, 'hurt people, hurt people', showing empathy, not with you and your family, which is where I think that we got to show empathy. But showing empathy to a guy who nearly killed hundreds of innocent children," Vance said.
El-Sayed had condemned the attack and later apologized for his wording, saying he never intended to justify the violence.
Elaine Mallon is a writer for Fox News Digital and Fox Business covering national politics.

### 36. ★★★☆☆ Dems Have an Opportunity to Force an End to the Iran War. Will They Take It? - The Intercept

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T09:57:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Dems Have an Opportunity to Force an End to the Iran War. Will They Take It?&nbsp;&nbsp;The Intercept

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., speaks during a news conference at the Capitol Building on July 23, 2026, in Washington, D.C.
For months, Democrats have forced Republicans to take vote after vote on President Donald Trump’s unpopular war on Iran. Now those votes could help Democrats retake Congress — and they will immediately have opportunities to follow through on their anti-war rhetoric.
Democratic leaders like House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York have been cagey about their strategy on Iran for the lame-duck period before the next Congress is sworn in on January 3. So far, months of Capitol Hill maneuvering have produced few concrete results. And one path to potential progress for ending the war is laden with risk: threatening a government shutdown.
Opponents of the war say that, with Democrats pushing for support based on their efforts to end to the war, they owe it to voters to put government spending on the line.
“It will certainly demonstrate whether Democrats are willing to stretch themselves and put up a fight and get uncomfortable,” said Hannah Morris, the vice president of government affairs for the liberal, pro-Israel group J Street, which opposes the conflict. “So much of the rhetoric has been about the Iran war.”
Congress Failed Again to Stop Trump’s Iran War. This Group Is Trying Through the Courts.
In the months since the conflict began on February 28, Democrats and the handful of Republicans opposed to the war have poured most of their energy into votes based on the 1973 War Powers Resolution , which limits the president’s power to wage war without congressional approval. In June, they succeeded in securing House and Senate passage of a war powers resolution, but the Trump administration has ignored the vote, arguing the law is unconstitutional.
ICE Raid on Ilhan Omar Shows How Dangerous Two Years of Flailing Trump Could Get
Trump Is Targeting Ilhan Omar. Where’s the Elite Media Outrage?
Lockheed Martin Showered Susan Collins With Cash as F-35 Program Hit Turbulence
Advocates still believe the Trump administration should be bound by the June resolution, but they concede that other paths may have more teeth.
If they really want to stop the war, Democrats will have to confront questions of timing, divisions within their own caucus, and the risk of a government shutdown.
One prime opportunity could be the December 11 expiration of a spending bill called the continuing resolution that has been funding the government since the last shutdown. Democrats could attempt to attach a rider to the bill prohibiting the use of any of its funds for the Iran war. If that bill does not pass both chambers, the government shuts down.
The process will provide Democrats with rare leverage before the next Congress is sworn in.
Congressional Democratic leaders have not said whether they are willing to risk a shutdown, however. Jeffries did not tip his hand when he was asked during a recent interview with CNN.
“Listen, we'll deal with the appropriations process when we deal with the appropriations process,” he said . “Right now, the mechanism that is in front of us will be to aggressively move war powers resolutions until we end this war in the best interest of the American people.”
Some Democratic candidates in hotly contested races have gone much further than Jeffries, zeroing in on the appropriations issue. Rep. Tom Barrett, of Michigan’s 7th Congressional District, was one of the few Republicans to vote for an Iran war powers resolution. But environmental activist Will Lawrence still attacked him at a recent debate on the issue of war funding.
Morris, who is part of a coalition of groups that have called on Congress to cut off funding for the war, said she is not sure how aggressively Democrats will be willing to pursue the issue during the lame-duck period.
“Will they be willing to shut down the government over this? I don’t have real answers. I think there are a lot of variables here,” she said.
Another option supported by many advocates is the pending debate over the National Defense Authorization Act, the annual Pentagon budget and policy bill. Critics of the war argue that Congress could attempt to insert a provision into the law cutting off authorization for the conflict.
Democrats should be willing to block the bill if it does not include “meaningful restrictions” on the administration’s ability to wage the war, said Hassan El-Tayyab, legislative director for Middle East Policy at the Friends Committee on National Legislation.
“That’s the mandate we’re seeing from the American people,” he said. “They shouldn't pass a massive defense bill while leaving the administration free to pursue yet another open-ended war in the Middle East.”
The defense bill is generally considered “must-pass” legislation on Capitol Hill, providing leverage for Senate Democrats who could block it with a filibuster. The office of Sen. Jack Reed, D-R.I., who plays a key role in the process as the top Democrat on the Senate Armed Services Committee, did not respond to a request for comment.
Falling short of either of those paths, Democrats could continue to try to pass more war powers resolutions. With their party poised for a dramatic shift in its favor in the midterm elections , Democrats could even try to exert pressure on Republican legislators to vote in favor of the resolutions, some opponents of the war believe.
We’re independent of corporate interests — and powered by members. Join us.
Original reporting. Fearless journalism. Delivered to you.

By signing up, I agree to receive emails from The Intercept and to the Privacy Policy and Terms of Use .
Original reporting. Fearless journalism. Delivered to you.
Democrats could also wait until January, when they are widely predicted to retake control of one or both chambers of Congress. Holding the gavel in the House or Senate will give them vastly expanded powers to set military spending and launch investigations into the conduct of the war.
One key Democrat, Rep. Adam Smith of Washington, told The Intercept in a statement he was willing to pursue multiple paths to stopping the war, but all of them face obstacles until the next Congress.
GOP Rep. Called Israeli–U.S. Defense Tech Integration “Dangerous.” The House Passed It Anyway.
“I am open to exploring options for using the NDAA or appropriations bills as a way to end the war,” said Smith, the ranking member of the House Armed Services Committee. “That effort would most likely require bipartisan support. Unfortunately, I am not confident that there is sufficient Republican will or support for such legislative efforts or to override an inevitable veto.”
Whether in this Congress or the next, cutting off spending for the war appears to be the best way for Democrats to force the Trump administration to negotiate with Iran, according to Brian Finucane, a former State Department official who serves as a senior adviser at the nonprofit International Crisis Group.
“If they are serious,” he said, “they should be willing to play hardball, and that would require using the power of the purse.”
What we’re seeing right now from Donald Trump is a full-on authoritarian takeover of the U.S. government.
Court orders are being ignored. MAGA loyalists have been put in charge of the military and federal law enforcement agencies. The Department of Government Efficiency has stripped Congress of its power of the purse. News outlets that challenge Trump have been banished or put under investigation.
Yet far too many are still covering Trump’s assault on democracy like politics as usual, with flattering headlines describing Trump as “unconventional,” “testing the boundaries,” and “aggressively flexing power.”
The Intercept has long covered authoritarian governments, billionaire oligarchs, and backsliding democracies around the world. We understand the challenge we face in Trump and the vital importance of press freedom in defending democracy.
We’re independent of corporate interests. Will you help us?
IT’S BEEN A DEVASTATING  year for journalism — the worst in modern U.S. history.
We have a president with utter contempt for truth aggressively using the government’s full powers to dismantle the free press. Corporate news outlets have cowered, becoming accessories in Trump’s project to create a post-truth America. Right-wing billionaires have pounced, buying up media organizations and rebuilding the information environment to their liking.
In this most perilous moment for democracy, The Intercept is fighting back. But to do so effectively, we need to grow.

### 37. ★★★☆☆ Intensifying drone attacks on ships in Black Sea bring war ever closer to Nato shores - The Guardian

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-18T05:00:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Intensifying drone attacks on ships in Black Sea bring war ever closer to Nato shores&nbsp;&nbsp;The Guardian

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The Wine Trader ship was struck in the territorial waters of Romania, near Sulina, on Thursday. Photograph: ARSVOM/AFP/Getty Images
The Wine Trader ship was struck in the territorial waters of Romania, near Sulina, on Thursday. Photograph: ARSVOM/AFP/Getty Images
Intensifying drone attacks on ships in Black Sea bring war ever closer to Nato shores
Six attacks in 10 days by suspected Russian drones mark dramatic escalation in maritime conflict
T he first the crew of the Bulgarian ferry Dioscuria knew of an attack on a cargo ship in their country’s exclusive economic zone in the Black Sea in the early hours of 6 October was a distant explosion 70 miles off the coastal town of Byala.
The Turkish-owned Alfa Watan, heading for the Romanian port of Sulina, had been struck by suspected Russian air and naval drones. Arriving on the scene, Dioscuria found an overturned lifeboat and lifejackets in the water. There was no sign, however, of Alfa Watan’s crew of 10.
Map showing location of ships attacked in the Black Sea’s EU waters in October Six days later drones struck another ship off the coast of Bulgaria, HS Dignity. Eleven crew members died.
By Thursday, the attacks had encroached on Romania’s airspace and territorial water. Two chemical tankers, Wine Trader and Yara J, were attacked and left burning. Seven more sailors were missing.
Four of the rescued sailors from HS Dignity on a Bulgarian navy rescue tug, which transported them to the port of Varna on Tuesday. Photograph: moreto.net/AP View image in fullscreen
Crew members from the Able, which was damaged in a drone attack, disembark from a Bulgarian border police vessel in Varna after a rescue operation. Photograph: Petko Momchilov/Reuters The attacks on six ships in 10 days – the Able and Chelsea-3 were also hit – marked a dramatic escalation in a maritime conflict that is pressing on Nato’s borders.
While global attention has focused on the risks for sailors travelling through the strait of Hormuz, in recent months it is the Black Sea that has emerged as the more dangerous passage, with more sailors lost in a week and a half than US service members killed in the entire Iran war.
Alfa Watan, which sank after a drone attack, sails in Istanbul, Turkey, the day before the incident. Photograph: Yoruk Isik/Reuters The maritime insurer and risk analyst Ambrey has warned that there is no end in sight to the threats to Black Sea cargo shipping, including grain and oil, and that insurance prices for passage of the waters have rocketed.
Tomas Alexa, a senior analyst at Ambrey, has noted a sharp increase in the tempo of attacks beginning in July. Russian forces are estimated to be responsible for 80% of them.
The recent loss of life, he suggests, has been exacerbated by “double tap” attacks involving aerial and surface drones, the latter often carrying up to half a tonne of explosive payloads. Smaller vessels that primarily use the Danube canal system have sunk rapidly.
“While attacks against merchant vessels in the Black Sea are not new, we are seeing an escalation both in terms of the number of attacks and in the geographical scope,” Alexa said.
The profile of vessels being attacked – that is, ships trading with Ukraine – had not changed, he added. “The Russian intent is clear, which is to degrade the export of Ukrainian grain and agricultural products.”
The Comoros-flagged chemical and oil tanker Yara J transits the Bosphorus strait in Istanbul, Turkey, last month. Photograph: Yoruk Isik/Reuters The result has been a precipitous fall in the number of vessels visiting Ukraine. Insurance rates have soared, and seafarers and operators have become increasingly reluctant to operate in the Black Sea basin, even on ships unconnected with Ukrainian cargoes.
The vast majority of the more than 60 fatalities of seafarers in the Black Sea since Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022 have occurred in the past year. In September the Joint War Committee, representing the London insurance market, moved to extend the high-risk areas of the Black Sea to include the whole of the basin, excluding the territorial waters of Turkey, Romania, Bulgaria and Georgia.
The threat is not confined to the Black Sea itself. Attacks on Ukraine’s Black Sea ports have also increased sharply in the past year.
A number of factors are driving the expansion and escalation of the conflict towards the shores of Nato countries, say experts. Russia has responded to a geographical expansion of Ukrainian strikes on its shadow fleet of oil tankers by intensifying strikes on Ukrainian agricultural cargoes – a key component of Kyiv’s economy.
As ships sought safer passage further from the coastlines, so the threat has changed: from naval mines to drones, with ever longer ranges.
Ursula von der Leyen, the European Commission president, said after the sinking of the Alfa Watan: “Commercial ships – and innocent ​crews – are being targeted, ​with lives already lost at sea.”
Demonstrators gather in Sofia, Bulgaria, on Thursday during a protest demanding clarification over recent Black Sea maritime attacks. Photograph: Hristo Vladev/Anadolu/Getty Images In a statement at the Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe, Col Joby Rimmer, a senior British military adviser, said: “Attacks that fail to distinguish between military objectives and civilian vessels, or that are expected to cause excessive incidental harm to civilians and civilian vessels in relation to the anticipated military advantage, may violate international humanitarian law and the UN convention on the law of the sea.
“Each attack raises the risk to crews of every nationality, drives up insurance costs and restricts the flow of food to those who depend on it. The UK is deeply concerned by the far-reaching consequences of successive crises across the global maritime domain. Stable and secure sea lanes are a global common good from which we all benefit.”
Operators have warned of the wider impact on trade in the Black Sea, including from Bulgarian ports. In a recent interview on Bulgarian television, the shipping agency owner Todor Georgiev warned the Black Sea was in danger of becoming a “dead zone”.
A vessel that was hit by a drone in the Black Sea this week. Photograph: Bulgarian Interior Ministry/Reuters “Whenever the Russians are caught having struck a ship with one of their drones, they claim the vessel was carrying weapons,” he said. “Weapons are not being transported to Ukraine via the Black Sea.”
Rumen Radev, Bulgaria’s prime minister, has described the attacks in the vicinity of his country’s territorial waters as “absolutely unacceptable”.
Bulgarian officials are calling for the EU and Nato to support the efforts to enhance security, while working with Turkey and Romania on increased regional security.
Although they have refused to attribute blame to Russia, officials have said there is “no logic” for Ukraine to attack its own shipments.
A cargo ship in the Black Sea on Thursday. Photograph: Nikolay Doychinov/AFP/Getty Images “We do not have clear and conclusive evidence to attribute responsibility to either side,” said Radev. “However, as I have already stated, the logic suggests that the outcome benefits the Russian Federation.”
As Russian strikes on shipping move ever closer to the borders of Nato members, they pose mounting security challenges to the mutual defence organisation, which is already confronting Moscow’s growing hybrid warfare across Europe. There is speculation that the affected Nato countries may be forced to invoke article 4 of the organisation’s treaty, a step below the article 5 invocation of mutual self defence.
Triggered twice already around the Ukraine war – in the immediate aftermath of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in 2022 and by Poland in 2025 after Russian drones violated Warsaw’s airspace – it calls for formal crisis discussions on a security threat.
On Thursday Mark Rutte, Nato’s secretary general, met Radev and Nicușor ⁠Dan, Romania’s president, to ​discuss ‌security ‌in the Black Sea. Rutte said: “Nato ​will continue to work closely with ⁠our allies in ​the Black Sea, ​adjusting ​our deterrence ​and ‌defence ​posture ​as needed.”
Another consequence of the escalating attacks is the renewed danger of a global food crisis. Ukraine and Russia are major exporters of grain, as is Romania.
Diners at a restaurant overlooking the harbour in Varna. Photograph: Nikolay Doychinov/AFP/Getty Images “We’re looking ​at the Black Sea as a global food ​artery,” said Oana Toiu, the Romanian foreign minister, at a security ​conference in Warsaw last week. “It is currently, unfortunately, the deadliest sea when it comes to the number of casualties of sailors ​in the last months. That means ‌we need to increase our ​investments in deterrence, defence.”
Damien Chevallier, the director of maritime safety at the UN’s International Maritime Organization, warned last month of the long-term impact on shipping caused by attacks linked to the conflicts in Ukraine and the Middle East.
“With rising casualties across the Black Sea, Red Sea and strait of Hormuz, being a seafarer has become a genuinely dangerous profession,” he said. “Seafarers are not trained to deal with threats from actions which are linked to wider security issues or geopolitical situations.
“What makes this moment genuinely unprecedented is not just the number of incidents, but the pattern behind them. Shipping has become a deliberate instrument of geopolitical leverage.”

### 39. ★★★☆☆ If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, These 5 Stocks Have a History of Holding Up - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 📈 市场

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, These 5 Stocks Have a History of Holding Up
Is a market crash coming? That's the question investors are asking as the S&P 500 (SNPINDEX: ^GSPC) gets closer to ending its fourth year in a row with double-digit gains.
Understandably, a market crash is a scary thought. However, it's part of investing. When you set up your portfolio, keep that in mind and create your stock mix accordingly. When you do your annual review, swap out different stocks based on where you see gaps. And if you haven't done that yet and anticipate a market crash on the horizon, you should make your move now.
Missed AI's "Act 1"? Act 2 Could Be 14x Bigger. Most investors think they missed the AI boat because they didn't buy Nvidia in 2005. But according to our analysts, we're only at the end of "Act 1"—the R&D phase. "Act 2" is the global rollout. Continue »
Although a market crash can affect even defensive and value stocks, some manage through better than others, and some actually thrive under adverse circumstances. Costco Wholesale (NASDAQ: COST), TJX Companies (NYSE: TJX), Coca-Cola (NYSE: KO), Procter & Gamble (NYSE: PG), and Walmart (NASDAQ: WMT) all have a history of holding up through market crashes.
What's more, they all pay dividends, and Coca-Cola, Procter & Gamble, and Walmart are Dividend Kings , meaning they've raised their dividends for at least 50 consecutive years. That's reliability under pressure. Here's how they performed in 2008, 2018, and 2022, when the S&P 500 lost value:
Costco's low prices are a draw all the time, but they become even more valuable under pressure, and that's why the market loves it during a downturn. As inflation persists, rising another 3.7% in September, Costco's sales growth has accelerated, reaching 13% year over year in September.
Image source: Getty Images. That's great news for Costco investors. Costco stock is neck and neck with the market, up 14% year to date, and if there's a correction or crash, it's likely to manage through .
Notice that in the last chart, Costco underperformed the S&P 500. However, it gained 49% the next year, nearly double the index's gain. Often, the resilience is in how quickly the stock bounces back after a downturn.
TJX is an off-price retail chain that buys overstock and off-season merchandise from designer brands. It works with 21,000 vendors and therefore can be very selective about what it buys.
The company sells products at 20% to 60% off retail prices in its 5,200 global stores, and it becomes a magnet for shoppers during periods of inflation or economic distress. Sales increased 4% year over year in the fiscal 2027 second quarter (ended Aug. 1), and earnings per share were up 24%.
TJX barely beat the market in 2008, and that's another example of a lag period -- it gained 81% in 2009, triple the market's rebound.
Coca-Cola is the world's largest beverage company, and the company has developed brands that people love and are willing to pay for. That gives it sticking power despite whatever's happening in the economy. It generally outperforms when the market's having a tough time, because investors know it has a resilient business, and because of its storied dividend, which it has raised for the past 64 years straight.
Coca-Cola stock has been outperforming this year despite the market's rise for similar reasons, and it's up a fantastic 40%, outperforming many hot tech stocks. Sales increased 7% year over year in the second quarter, and EPS rose 16%.
Procter & Gamble makes many of the world's leading household and hygiene brands, such as Tide laundry detergent, Oral-B oral care, and Bounty paper towels. People are willing to pay extra for these products because they feel they're getting good value, and they're essentials people buy no matter what. Procter & Gamble stock soared early in the pandemic, for example, when people were loading up on toilet paper (it makes the Charmin brand).
Procter & Gamble has one of the longest dividend-raise track records, at 70 years, another reason it's valuable in a downturn.
Finally, Walmart is the largest retailer in the world, with almost 11,000 stores, and it reaches 90% of the U.S. population within 10 miles. Its discount prices are even more attractive when people are penny-pinching.
Image source: Walmart. Today, it's also reaching more people with its e-commerce channels. E-commerce sales increased 23% year over year in the 2027 fiscal second quarter (ended July 31), driving total sales growth of 5.9%.
Walmart is also a Dividend King, having raised its dividend annually for the past 53 years.
The Motley Fool Stock Advisor analyst team just identified what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy now… and Walmart wasn't one of them. The 10 stocks that made the cut could produce monster returns in the coming years.
Consider when Netflix made this list on December 17, 2004... if you invested $1,000 at the time of our recommendation, you'd have $380,363 !* Or when Nvidia made this list on April 15, 2005... if you invested $1,000 at the time of our recommendation, you'd have $1,315,732 !*
Now, it's worth noting Stock Advisor's total average return is 919% — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 211% for the S&P 500. Don't miss the latest top 10 list, available with Stock Advisor , and join an investing community built by individual investors for individual investors.

### 40. ★★★☆☆ If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, History Says This Strategy Can Cut Your Portfolio's Recovery Time in Half - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T18:15:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, History Says This Strategy Can Cut Your Portfolio's Recovery Time in Half&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motley Fool

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The last few years have been absolutely fantastic for stock investors. The bull market of the last four years has pushed the S&P 500 ( ^GSPC -1.09% ) 116% higher while the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite ( ^IXIC -2.15% ) is up an even more impressive 156%.
While the bull market has been strong and supported by stellar earnings growth and improving outlooks, many investors can&#x27;t help but notice that stock market valuations are particularly high. That&#x27;s not a sustainable position for stocks: Either earnings have to grow fast enough to bring valuations lower, or stock prices have to come down.
In fact, it&#x27;s a practical inevitability that stock prices crash 20% or more in the future. The markets move in cycles, and eventually, some event will trigger a big sell-off in the stock market. If you want to position your portfolio to recover in less than half the time it takes for others, here&#x27;s exactly what you should do.
It doesn&#x27;t take any groundbreaking portfolio strategy to produce rapid recoveries in the event of a stock market crash. Investors can hold a simple 60/40 stock/bond portfolio with periodic rebalancing and achieve superior results in maintaining portfolio stability.
Since 1988, the 60/40 portfolio has recovered from drawdowns of 20% or more in just 11 months on average, according to data compiled by J.P. Morgan analysts. Meanwhile, the S&P 500 took an average of 24 months to recover, even with dividends reinvested.
It&#x27;s worth noting that the 60/40 portfolio only stands out against an all-stock portfolio in terms of recovery when the portfolio drops at least 20%. Smaller drops don&#x27;t exhibit much difference in how long it takes the portfolio to recover. That said, that&#x27;s exactly the kind of protection an investor should hope for from bonds : a stabilizing force amid big long drops without providing too much drag amid shorter pullbacks.
The stability of the 60/40 portfolio is further evidenced by the likelihood that it will produce a positive return over any given five- or 10-year period. Since 1989, the 60/40 portfolio has produced a positive return 99% of the time over any five-year period and 100% of the time over 10 years. The S&P 500 alone only achieves positive returns 87% of the time over five years and 93% of the time over 10 years.
Maintaining a 60/40 ratio between stocks and bonds in your portfolio requires periodic rebalancing. When more volatile stocks crash, you&#x27;ll end up with a lower ratio of stocks to bonds in your portfolio. You&#x27;ll have to sell some bonds and buy some stocks to return the portfolio to the 60/40 balance.
This act of rebalancing means you automatically buy stocks when they go down. The reverse is also true; you&#x27;ll sell stocks when they climb higher or if bonds decline. That means you can be opportunistic in buying and selling stocks , which ultimately produces more stable returns and helps a portfolio recover quickly after a crash.
While some have concerns about whether bonds can truly act as a good diversifying asset for stocks after both asset classes fell sharply in 2022, that situation was more of an anomaly than a new normal. Bonds produced positive returns in 21 of the last 25 years in which the S&P 500 declined. As such, investors should remain confident that bonds can provide diversification when it matters most.
With bond prices sinking lower in 2026, right now could be an opportune time to increase your allocation to the asset class. The Vanguard Total Bond Market ETF ( BND +0.01% ) , which tracks the Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond index, currently offers a yield to maturity of 5.5%.
Splitting a portfolio between the Vanguard bond ETF and the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF ( VOO -1.10% ) can provide significant downside protection and the potential to recover from a market crash more quickly with the help of rebalancing into stocks.
However, investors should understand that bonds have more limited upside than stocks, and they could drag down performance somewhat over the long run. On average, the 60/40 portfolio has produced an annualized return of 9.4% since 1950, versus 11.7% for the S&P 500. That&#x27;s the price of downside protection. But with bond values sinking lower this year, that downside protection doesn&#x27;t look as expensive as in the past.

Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 Gets a Warm Steam Welcome With 90% Positive Reviews
Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 is off to an unusually good start on Steam. The campaign early access went live for pre-order customers on October 16, and at the time of writing, 1,630 of the game's 1,812 Steam user reviews are positive, giving it a "Very Positive" score of 90%. Insider Gaming first spotted the trend when the game had around 900 reviews and a SteamDB rating of 81.15%, which already made it the best-received Call of Duty on Steam since 2015's Black Ops III. The review count has more than doubled since then, and the positive share has climbed to 85.78% at the time of writing. Of course, these are early days. The current reviews come from players who pre-ordered to play the campaign a week early, so they are probably the most enthusiastic part of the audience.
Still, the last few releases fared much worse by comparison. SteamDB rates Black Ops 7 at 38.71%, Black Ops 6 at 45.75%, Modern Warfare III at 48.19%, and Modern Warfare II at 47.10%, while Black Ops III still leads the modern games at 84.36%. SteamDB's formula pulls games with fewer reviews closer to 50%, so as more positive reviews come in, Modern Warfare 4 could leave Black Ops III in the rearview mirror fairly soon. Multiplayer and DMZ only arrive with the full launch on October 23, and the score could look quite different once the wider player base jumps in. PC players should also keep in mind that TPM 2.0 and Secure Boot are mandatory to jump into the action.

Iran-backed militias funnel money to IRGC amid economic crisis, CENTCOM commander Cooper says
Cooper said the financial pressure had become severe enough that Iran-aligned militias were reportedly withholding payments from their own fighters to funnel money back to the IRGC.
US CENTRAL COMMAND chief Adm. Brad Cooper has played a pivotal role in shaping American military strategy toward Iran and the wider Middle East. Here, he testifies before lawmakers in Washington in May.
Iran-backed militias including Hezbollah , Hamas, and groups in Iraq have been redirecting money to Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) as Tehran faces mounting economic pressure after nine months of war, US Central Command (CENTCOM) chief Adm. Brad Cooper said in a video statement released on Friday.
The United States’ naval blockade has cost Iran an estimated $25 billion in lost oil revenue, he said. Additionally, CENTCOM and the US Treasury Department froze $350 million in Iranian cryptocurrency assets in recent weeks.
Iran-backed militias withholding payments to their own troops to funnel money to IRGC
Cooper noted that the financial pressure had become severe enough for Iran-aligned militias to be said to be withholding payments from their own fighters so that the struggling IRGC could pay its troops.
“The financial strain on Iran’s military has become so significant that there are reports about Iran-aligned militia groups across the region withholding payments for their soldiers in order to funnel money back to the IRGC, which also isn’t paying its troops,” Cooper asserted.
The CENTCOM chief attributed increased Iranian attacks on shipping in the Strait of Hormuz to what he described as desperation within the IRGC. He said that the waterway remained open, with 400 ships and 100 million barrels of oil passing through it over the previous week.
CENTCOM has also mostly paused strikes on Iranian military positions threatening the Strait of Hormuz in recent weeks.
The Trump administration has continued attempts to place economic pressure on Iran to secure an agreement.
A Friday Axios report said that the White House and Qatari mediators were awaiting Iran’s response to a US counterproposal aimed at ending the war.
An American official cited by the media outlet said the White House ordered the pause to avoid escalating the war before the US midterm elections, as US President Donald Trump’s approval rating had dipped to 38%, according to NBC News polling.
The Axios report said that US officials believe that the Islamic Republic is increasing attacks on ships transiting the strait in an effort to raise oil prices and increase pressure on Trump ahead of the midterm elections.

### 49. ★★★☆☆ Google Teases Synaptics Edge AI Chip, Cuts Power 40% [2026] - shattered.io

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-18T02:05:21+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Google Teases Synaptics Edge AI Chip, Cuts Power 40% [2026]&nbsp;&nbsp;shattered.io

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Google Teases Synaptics Edge AI Chip, Cuts Power 40% [2026]
Google used its annual Research@ event in Mountain View, California, on October 14, 2026, to preview a new low-power AI chip built with Synaptics, setting up a formal unveiling at Synaptics' Tech Day in San Jose on October 20. The chip pairs Google's Coral NPU M2 with Synaptics' Torq NPU and Arm CPU cores, and the companies say it cuts energy use by 40% compared with existing edge AI processors. Synaptics CEO Rahul Patel said the chip will not stay confined to lab demos. According to a report from R&D World , Patel told attendees it will also be built into Google devices, though neither company named which products or when they would ship.
The preview lands at a moment when chipmakers are racing to put AI inference directly on wearables, smart glasses, and industrial sensors instead of routing every request to the cloud. Google and Synaptics are betting that sub-6-milliwatt inference, paired with an open compiler stack, can pull developers away from the proprietary microcontroller software they have relied on for years. The timing also puts pressure on rivals already shipping edge AI silicon at scale, from Qualcomm's Snapdragon line to smaller microcontroller specialists like Ambiq, NXP, and STMicroelectronics.
Google Research's Research@ event is the company's internal showcase for work still moving from lab to product, and this year's edition gave Synaptics a stage to describe a chip that had been in development for roughly 18 to 24 months. The device belongs to Synaptics' Astra line of AI-native processors and microcontrollers, a product family the company has built specifically for local, low-power AI workloads rather than general-purpose computing.
Patel used the Mountain View appearance to lay out the chip's capabilities without disclosing full specifications, a sequencing choice that let Google tie the story to its broader research narrative before Synaptics controls the commercial unveiling six days later. The chip can process voice, vision, motion, and contextual sensing data simultaneously, and it adds GPU and multimedia processing along with hardware-rooted security, according to the companies' public statements cited in a report from SBS .
The headline number from the preview is a claimed 40% cut in energy use for Google's Coral NPU M2, the successor to the Coral neural processing unit Google introduced the prior year. Reporting tied to the announcement, including coverage from ChosunBiz , describes M2 running on-device inference at an average power draw of less than 6 milliwatts. Neither Google nor Synaptics has specified the exact workloads used for that comparison, or which prior-generation processor served as the baseline, so the 40% figure should be read as a vendor claim rather than an independently benchmarked result.
That caveat matters because power-efficiency claims in the chip industry are notoriously workload-dependent. A processor that looks dramatically more efficient on a simple keyword-spotting task can lose much of its edge on continuous multimodal sensing, which is exactly the use case Google and Synaptics are targeting. Until Synaptics publishes a full datasheet, outside engineers will not be able to verify the 40% number against a defined test suite.
Reported efficiency claim: 40% less energy vs. prior-generation processors
Comparison baseline disclosed: No (workload and reference chip not specified)
Synaptics did not simply license Google's silicon design and ship it unchanged. The Astra chip combines the Coral NPU M2 with Synaptics' own Torq NPU and standard Arm CPU cores, giving the company a path to differentiate on top of Google's open accelerator work. That architecture lets Synaptics target a wide span of constrained devices, from battery-powered wearables to fixed industrial cameras, with one underlying platform rather than a separate chip for each category.
A less flashy but arguably more consequential part of the announcement is software. Synaptics and Google Research spent roughly 18 months building an open-source compiler on top of MLIR, the Multi-Level Intermediate Representation framework, so the chip can run standard machine learning frameworks and model formats rather than locking developers into a proprietary toolchain. Patel framed this as a break from the walled-garden software that has defined microcontroller development for years, a point echoed in R&D World's account of his remarks. Developers can already prototype against the underlying Coral NPU using Coralboard, a development platform Synaptics launched earlier this year that pairs the NPU with Astra processors ahead of the full chip's release.
Coral's first milestone was a front end built on RISC-V, the open instruction set architecture that has gained ground across the chip industry as an alternative to proprietary cores. M2 marks the second milestone and adds what Google describes as the first Coral vector processor. Google Research has also said its next design, M3, is already complete and adds native 32-bit floating-point support so the chip can run Gemma 3 270M, a compact 270-million-parameter language model, directly on-device. Neither company has given a shipping date for M3-based silicon, which means the chip debuting this month is already one generation behind Google's internal roadmap.
Rahul Patel's Pitch: From Lab Bench to Google Hardware
Patel's most direct claim from the Research@ appearance was that the chip will not stay in the lab and will also be built into Google devices, a statement widely picked up across outlets covering the preview. He also highlighted the microcontroller's support for high-density DRAM, describing memory capacity as a source of scalability for future products built on the same platform. Synaptics plans to formally unveil the chip at its Tech Day event on October 20 at 1151 McKay Drive in San Jose, which the company has billed as including a noon press and analyst briefing followed by a demonstration showcase.
What Patel did not say is just as notable. He declined to identify which Google hardware lines would adopt the chip or offer any release window, leaving a gap between the marketing claim and a verifiable product commitment. That gap is where skepticism from hardware analysts tends to concentrate, since chipmakers routinely tout future design wins that take years, or never materialize, to reach shelves.
Why Google Keeps Investing in Edge Silicon Partnerships
Google's data center strategy has long rested on designing its own Tensor Processing Units alongside the models that run on them, and the company is now pushing the same co-design philosophy to the edge. The logic, as described in reporting on the Research@ event, is that existing edge chips consume too much energy and respond too slowly for always-on, ambient AI experiences that keep personal data on the device instead of sending it to the cloud. A fragmented market of proprietary microcontroller designs compounds the problem by making it harder for developers to build once and deploy across multiple vendors' hardware.
That framing explains why Google chose a partnership model rather than building its own edge chip from scratch. Synaptics brings decades of microcontroller and sensor-fusion experience along with existing customer relationships in wearables and industrial equipment, while Google supplies the Coral accelerator design and compiler work it has already validated internally.
Billy Rutledge Makes the Case for Co-Designed Hardware
When Google and Synaptics first announced their collaboration on edge AI for the Internet of Things, Billy Rutledge, Director of Systems Research at Google Research, laid out why the two companies paired up in the first place. "Synaptics' embrace of open software and tools and proven AI hardware makes the Astra portfolio a natural fit for our ML core as we ramp to meet the uniquely challenging power, performance, cost, and space requirements of Edge AI devices," Rutledge said in the companies' joint statement announcing the partnership.
Rutledge closed that announcement with a line that reads differently now that the chip has reached a public preview. "We look forward to working together to bring our capabilities to the broad market," he said. Nearly two years of engineering separate that statement from this month's Research@ demonstration, which is roughly in line with the 18-to-24-month development window Patel cited for the project.
Synaptics spokesperson Patrick Mannion gave the clearest public explanation of the division of labor between Google's open core and Synaptics' own hardware in remarks made around CES 2025. "What we're doing is taking their open ML core and adding our own hardware engine as an enhancement," Mannion said. He added that the goal was tuning that shared foundation for the specific operators Synaptics' engine supports: "We're making that open core run better for the set of operators that we support in the engine."
Mannion also confirmed the architectural lineage underpinning the whole platform in a brief, direct answer about the subsystem's design. "It's RISC-V-based," he said, a detail that lines up with Google's own description of Coral's first milestone as a RISC-V front end before M2 added the vector processor on top of it.
Target Devices: Wearables, Smart Glasses, and the Industrial Floor
The use cases Google and Synaptics are chasing span two very different markets. On the consumer side, smart glasses, watches, and connected cameras need to recognize speech, gestures, and context without draining a small battery in a few hours. On the industrial side, vision and voice systems on factory floors need similar always-on sensing but with tighter requirements around reliability and hardware-rooted security, since these devices often sit on networks that also touch safety-critical equipment.
Covering both markets with one chip platform is an ambitious bet. It means Synaptics has to hit aggressive power targets for battery-powered wearables while also meeting the durability and security expectations of industrial buyers, all on the same underlying silicon. If the company pulls it off, Astra could become a default reference design that OEMs reach for instead of commissioning custom edge AI silicon.
This announcement did not come out of nowhere. Google's interest in specialized AI silicon traces back to a 2017 Google research paper on its first-generation Tensor Processing Unit, which reported performance-per-watt gains of 30 to 80 times over the Intel Haswell CPUs and Nvidia K80 GPUs it was benchmarked against in production neural-network inference workloads, per R&D World's account of the comparison. That data-center work eventually fed into Google's push toward edge-specific silicon, starting with early Coral development boards and continuing through a 2023 partnership with Qualcomm on a RISC-V wearable platform for Wear OS.
The Synaptics collaboration builds directly on that foundation. Google and Synaptics announced their partnership in January 2025, Synaptics launched the Coralboard development platform earlier in 2026, and the companies previewed the resulting Astra chip at Research@ this October, ahead of the formal Tech Day unveiling.
2017 Google publishes first-generation TPU research paper citing 30-80x performance-per-watt gains
2023 Google and Qualcomm announce a RISC-V wearable platform for Wear OS
January 2025 Google Research and Synaptics publicly announce their edge AI collaboration
Early 2026 Synaptics launches Coralboard, pairing the Coral NPU with Astra processors for developers
October 14, 2026 Google previews the Coral NPU M2-based Astra chip at Research@ in Mountain View
October 20, 2026 Synaptics plans a formal unveiling at Tech Day in San Jose
Competitive Landscape: Astra vs. Qualcomm, Ambiq, NXP, and STMicroelectronics
Synaptics is not entering an empty market. Qualcomm remains the volume leader in AI-capable edge silicon, having shipped more than 800 million AI-capable chips in 2025 according to industry data compiled by Axis Intelligence, with Snapdragon platforms using Hexagon NPUs rated at up to 45 TOPS for on-device inference. That positions Qualcomm at the high-performance end of the market, aimed at smartphones and premium wearables that can absorb more power draw than the ultra-constrained devices Synaptics is targeting.
Ambiq, NXP, and STMicroelectronics compete in a different tier, focused on ultra-low-power microcontrollers, embedded processing, and sensor-heavy industrial and automotive applications. Synaptics is trying to carve out space between these two groups, pitching Astra as a highly integrated, ultra-low-power platform that can still handle the multimodal sensing workloads usually reserved for higher-power chips like Qualcomm's.
Company Primary Edge AI Platform Notable Metric Market Position
Synaptics Astra (Coral NPU M2 + Torq NPU + Arm cores) Claimed <6mW inference, 40% less energy Ultra-low-power multimodal sensing
Qualcomm Snapdragon (Hexagon NPU) Up to 45 TOPS, 800M+ AI-capable chips shipped in 2025 High-performance mobile and wearable AI
Ambiq Apollo microcontroller line Ultra-low-power MCU focus Battery-constrained sensor devices
NXP i.MX / eIQ embedded processors Broad embedded and automotive portfolio Industrial and automotive edge processing
STMicroelectronics STM32 microcontrollers with AI extensions Established industrial sensor base Industrial and automotive sensor fusion
The timing of Google and Synaptics' preview lines up with a market that analysts expect to grow quickly. Grand View Research estimates, cited in Axis Intelligence's 2026 edge AI statistics roundup, value the edge AI chip market at $27.3 billion in 2025, growing to $36.2 billion in 2026. The same estimate projects the market reaching $291.8 billion by 2033, implying a compound annual growth rate of roughly 34.7%. Those figures cover the full edge AI chip category, not Synaptics or Google specifically, but they explain why a company like Synaptics is willing to spend nearly two years co-engineering silicon with a software giant rather than going it alone.
2025 $27.3 billion Grand View Research, via Axis Intelligence
2026 $36.2 billion (projected) Grand View Research, via Axis Intelligence
2033 $291.8 billion (projected) Grand View Research, via Axis Intelligence
2025-2033 CAGR Approximately 34.7% Grand View Research, via Axis Intelligence
If that growth curve holds, the edge AI chip market would more than triple in size between now and the end of the decade. That kind of expansion tends to pull in more entrants, not fewer, which raises the odds that Synaptics will face new ultra-low-power competitors within the next two or three product cycles rather than a stable five-company field.
For a chip generating this much coverage, the list of unconfirmed details is long. Neither company has disclosed the transistor count, the manufacturing process node, total on-chip memory capacity, a full-chip TOPS rating, the complete list of supported model architectures, pricing, expected production volume, or the first Google device that will actually ship with the chip inside it. The 40% power claim and the sub-6-milliwatt inference figure are the only hard performance numbers in circulation, and both come without a disclosed testing methodology.
That gap between marketing narrative and verifiable specification is normal for a pre-launch teaser, but it means most of the real analysis has to wait for October 20. Readers and investors treating this preview as a finished product announcement are getting ahead of what Google and Synaptics have actually confirmed.
Synaptics' Tech Day event in San Jose is where the preview either turns into a credible product or stalls as another unfulfilled chip tease. The company has described a noon press and analyst briefing followed by a demonstration showcase, which suggests working silicon will be on hand rather than simulations or reference designs alone. Analysts will be watching for a full TOPS rating covering the whole chip, confirmed pricing and sampling timelines for OEMs, and ideally a named lead customer or product category beyond the general "Google devices" language Patel used at Research@.
The compiler story also deserves scrutiny at the event. An open, MLIR-based toolchain is only as useful as its documentation and the breadth of model formats it actually supports on day one. If Synaptics ships the chip with a thin software stack and tells developers to wait for tooling updates, the open-compiler pitch will ring hollow regardless of how efficient the underlying silicon turns out to be.
Synaptics will likely disclose a full-chip TOPS rating and manufacturing process node at Tech Day on October 20, since withholding both details through a second public appearance would undercut the credibility of the power-efficiency claims.
Google will probably stay vague about which of its own devices will use the chip for at least one more product cycle, consistent with Patel's refusal to name specific hardware at Research@.
Qualcomm, NXP, or STMicroelectronics are likely to respond with their own ultra-low-power NPU announcements within the next two to three quarters, given how directly Synaptics is positioning Astra against the gap between their product tiers.
The open MLIR compiler approach could pressure other microcontroller vendors to loosen their proprietary software ecosystems, a shift that would play out gradually over 2027 rather than immediately.
If Grand View Research's $36.2 billion 2026 market estimate for edge AI chips proves accurate, expect more detailed Astra revenue guidance to show up in Synaptics' next earnings commentary rather than in marketing materials.
It is a low-power AI processor co-designed by Synaptics and Google Research, combining Google's Coral NPU M2 with Synaptics' own Torq NPU and Arm CPU cores. It belongs to Synaptics' Astra line of AI-native processors and microcontrollers.
When was the chip previewed and when is the full launch?
Google previewed the chip at its Research@ event in Mountain View on October 14, 2026. Synaptics plans to formally unveil it at its Tech Day event in San Jose on October 20, 2026.
Synaptics and Google say the Coral NPU M2 uses 40% less energy than existing processors and can run on-device inference at an average draw of less than 6 milliwatts. Neither company has disclosed the specific baseline chip or workload used for that comparison.
Synaptics CEO Rahul Patel said the chip will be built into Google devices, but neither company has named specific products or given a shipping timeline.
Target applications include wearables, smart glasses, connected cameras, and industrial vision and voice systems, which is any device where cloud-dependent AI processing adds too much latency or drains too much battery.
Qualcomm's Snapdragon platforms use Hexagon NPUs rated at up to 45 TOPS and shipped in more than 800 million AI-capable chips in 2025, targeting higher-power smartphones and premium wearables. Synaptics is targeting an even lower power tier aimed at multimodal sensing on far more constrained devices.
Google has said its next Coral design, M3, is already complete and adds native 32-bit floating-point support to run Gemma 3 270M, a 270-million-parameter language model, directly on-device. No shipping date for M3-based silicon has been announced.

TSMC vs. ASML: Which AI Expansion Demands More Cash?
TSMC and ASML's latest results reveal two very different ways to finance the same chip expansion. Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company Limited (NYSE: TSM ) builds and operates fabrication plants; ASML Holding N.V. (NASDAQ: ASML ) supplies the lithography equipment those factories require. The two rank second and sixth in our 10 Best AI Infrastructure Stocks to Buy for the Long Term.
TSMC vs. ASML: Which AI Expansion Demands More Cash? Image: TSMC
TSMC's October 15 results showed third-quarter revenue of NT$1.494 trillion and capital expenditure of NT$443.21 billion. That means 29.7% of sales went back into capital assets. Operating cash flow of NT$919.77 billion still covered that investment, leaving NT$476.56 billion after capital spending.
ASML's October 14 report showed €11.662 billion of sales and €447.2 million spent on property, equipment and intangible assets, or 3.8% of revenue. The gap reflects business structure: ASML's customers bear the cost of building and operating the fabs. Supplier investment also sits outside ASML's own capital expenditure, so this compares corporate cash commitments rather than the entire manufacturing chain.
That lower direct investment burden favors ASML for investors prioritizing expansion with less company-owned fixed capital. TSMC's counterargument is control over scarce advanced manufacturing capacity, supported by a 58.7% operating margin. Its larger investment bill creates productive capacity, provided customers keep those factories busy.
ASML generated €11.798 billion of operating cash flow, but €7.009 billion came from changes in assets and liabilities. Subtract that inflow and the €447.2 million investment bill and the residual is €4.341 billion. This is a diagnostic calculation, not a forecast or a complete normalized cash-flow estimate. Customer payment timing can make one quarter look exceptionally strong.
TSMC faces utilization, construction and geopolitical risks; ASML faces delayed equipment acceptance, order timing and export restrictions. Lower capital intensity does not by itself establish a cheaper stock: the price paid and sustainable earnings still determine the investment case. Our return analysis tests which growth and exit-valuation assumptions can reverse the TSMC-ASML choice .
Hedge-fund ownership in Q2 2026 rose to 249 from 234 for TSMC and to 140 from 133 for ASML. Fisher increased shares 2% and 3%, respectively. Those holdings preceded the October results. ASML's September 30 short interest was 0.37% of float.
ASML requires much less direct capital spending per revenue dollar. TSMC currently generates enough operating cash to finance its much larger program. For a long-term investor, the deciding evidence is sustained cash after investment: ASML must maintain collections as equipment ships, while TSMC must turn new capacity into durable utilization and profit.
While we acknowledge the potential of TSM and ASML as investments, we believe certain other AI stocks offer greater upside potential and carry less downside risk. If you're looking for an extremely undervalued AI stock that also stands to benefit significantly from Trump-era tariffs and the onshoring trend, see our free report on the best short-term AI stock .
Disclosure: None. Follow Insider Monkey on Google News .

### 52. ★★★☆☆ Stock market after midterms: S&P 500 up all 19 times - Opening Bell Daily

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T22:17:37+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Stock market after midterms: S&amp;P 500 up all 19 times&nbsp;&nbsp;Opening Bell Daily

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Stock market after midterms: S&P 500 up all 19 times
Stock market after midterms: S&P 500 rose all 19 times since 1950
Stock market after midterms: S&P 500 rose all 19 times since 1950
Stocks have gone up in the year after every midterm election since 1950. The S&P 500 gained an average of 15.1% in the 12 months following each of the last 19 midterm votes, and not one of those stretches ended lower.
That&#x27;s one of the cleanest calendar patterns on Wall Street. The median gain was 14.5%, so a couple of monster years aren&#x27;t doing all the work.
Runners-up, up 27.1% after 1994 and up 25.1% after 1990
Worst, up 1.1% after 1986, a window that took in the October 1987 crash
The gains tend to show up after the vote because the run-up is usually bumpy. In midterm years since 1950, the S&P 500&#x27;s biggest peak-to-trough drop within the year has averaged 17.5%, the deepest of any year in the four-year presidential cycle .
Those lows often landed in late summer or early fall, ahead of Election Day. Once the results are in, one big source of uncertainty is gone, and stocks have historically run from there.
The short-term pattern leans positive, but it&#x27;s weaker. From Election Day to December 31, the S&P 500 rose in 13 of the 19 midterm years, with an average gain of 2.6%.
2018 was the worst of that bunch, down 9.0% by December 31 after a slide into the Christmas Eve low. The index was still up 11.7% a year after that vote.
The full quarter looks better. The S&P 500 has averaged a 6.6% gain in the fourth quarter of midterm years , finishing higher in 16 of 19.
2026 is a midterm year, and Americans vote on Tuesday, November 3.
2026 broke the usual midterm script. The S&P 500 set a record close of 7,818.93 on October 6 and was up 11.9% for the year as of October 16. Its deepest drop of 2026 through that date was 9.1%, about half the midterm-year average.
For a deeper look at what tends to follow the vote, see post-midterm returns in three charts .
History says the hard part of a midterm year usually comes before the ballots are counted, not after.
