### 53. ★★☆☆☆ Photos show luxury boat with 50m pool and water slides near Kim Jong Un's summer retreat

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T22:09:50+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Satellite images give clues about how Kim spends his holidays - something far out of reach for most North Koreans.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

By Soram Cheon ,Â  BBC Korean Â  and Â  Aghnia Adzkia ,Â  East Asia Visual Journalism
An 80m (263ft) luxury vessel has been repeatedly spotted in satellite images off North Korea&#x27;s Wonsan coast from July to mid-October this year.
What stands out is the 50m swimming pool along the deck, with a cluster of waterslides at one end.
After sitting idle for months at a maintenance facility, it relocated in early July to waters off a strip of coastline known as the Kalma Peninsula.
This is close to Wonsan, a seaside resort with a sprawling villa used by North Korean leader Kim Jong Un and his family.
It was not the only large vessel on the move. A handful of others were also repeatedly seen near Kim&#x27;s villa, in satellite images analysed by the BBC.
The vessels are widely believed to be used by Kim and his family. Former North Korean officials, defectors and experts say it would be difficult for anyone unconnected to the regime&#x27;s leadership to own or freely use vessels of this size.
"In winter, they would keep them at the shipyard. When summer came, they would bring them out and move them to Kim Jong Un&#x27;s villa or towards Kalma, and use them for swimming and recreation," says Ri Jong Ho.
Ri was once an official at the Workers&#x27; Party&#x27;s Office 39, which is believed to be involved in raising and managing foreign currency for the leadership.
The wealth of those who sit at the top of the secretive North Korean dictatorship is just as hard to pin down as the country&#x27;s economic figures.
The United Nations and others rely on estimates to classify North Korea as one of the world&#x27;s poorest countries, with an especially large military budget that funds a sanctioned nuclear programme.
Most North Koreans, according to a 2025 UN report, live under "systematic, widespread and gross human rights violations" with total state control, widespread forced labour, and chronic food shortages that leave an estimated 40% of the population undernourished.
But the regime has long been known to live in luxury.
We don&#x27;t know the cost of the vessels seen in the satellite imagery, but comparable luxury yachts run from tens of millions to over $100m (Â£75m). Kim&#x27;s much smaller, 29-metre Princess yacht was previously estimated to be worth about $7m.
While vessel locations alone cannot definitively establish the family&#x27;s whereabouts, their movements - which overlap with gaps in Kim&#x27;s public appearances - point to activities that would otherwise remain out of public view.
Wonsan is where Kim Jong Un spent part of his childhood with his mother, according to biographers and intelligence reports, and one expert told the BBC the city has been associated with the family since his father Kim Jong Il&#x27;s rule.
A defector who served for 13 years guarding residences used by then leader Kim Jong Il - through the 1990s to the early 2000s - told the BBC the villas around Wonsan were used as a retreat by the elite. He recalls seeing dozens of children and teens waterskiing during summer.
North Korean state media rarely publishes reports about Kim being on holiday, instead framing such visits as official inspections or guidance. But defectors interviewed by the BBC appeared to be aware that the family spent holidays in Wonsan, suggesting that at least some North Koreans knew about it.
North Korean state media also claims Wonsan is home to high-rise hotels, restaurants, shops, sports facilities and water parks, and can accommodate nearly 20,000 visitors.
State media and Russian outlets have published images of small groups of Russian tourists visiting the Wonsan Kalma resort, which opened in July 2025. Although developed to attract foreign visitors, the resort is also open to local tourists.
Image caption, North Korean tourists on a beach in Kalma
A South Korean news report, citing an unnamed source, says some locals were offered four-night tour packages for around $100, a substantial sum for most North Koreans. The country&#x27;s per capita GDP - around $667 in 2024, according to the UN - is a small fraction of South Korea&#x27;s $36,227.
For the majority of North Koreans, holidays are out of reach. And while a growing middle class does exist, they might not have much time for leisure.
Defectors told the BBC that workers, office employees and cooperative farmers are legally entitled to 14 days of annual leave, but this is rarely followed.
Taking consecutive days off to travel is unusual. People typically take weekend trips or travel during national holidays instead of designated "summer holidays".
Even if leave is granted, travel itself is restricted, with official permission required for people to travel outside their city or county. Moving freely is sometimes as much a luxury as the holiday itself.
Image caption, Ordinary North Koreans struggle to earn a decent living
The BBC tracked four large vessels around Wonsan, measuring approximately 50m, 55m, 60m and 80m. All four vessels repeatedly appeared around Kim&#x27;s Wonsan villa this year.
The largest vessel showed the clearest activity, moving from the dock to the waters off the Kalma Peninsula, where it has been since July.
Two of the other vessels - 50m and 55m - were repeatedly seen until the end of August around Tae and Sin islands, close to Wonsan.
Satellite images also showed four smaller boats alongside the 50m vessel.
Image caption, The 50m vessel was seen in waters near Tae island on 4 July 2026.
Satellite imagery expert Jeong Seong-hak said the smaller boats ferried people, fuel and supplies to the larger vessel, adding that small islands like Tae and Sin offered "secluded maritime retreats where privacy can be maintained away from public view".
The 60m vessel was repeatedly seen near Kim&#x27;s Wonsan villa until as recently as last week.
Though North Korea has never publicly disclosed the vessels&#x27; official ownership, BBC&#x27;s analysis of historical satellite imagery found a large vessel with the same distinctive characteristics in Wonsan in 2002, when Kim Jong Il ruled North Korea.
Former NBA star Dennis Rodman, who visited the North in 2013, told The Sun that he had spent much of his seven-day visit with Kim Jong Un around Wonsan, where they went jet-skiing and horse riding.
He also described a 60m vessel they used as a cross between a ferry and a Disney boat. It is not clear if this is the same 60m boat pictured in the above satellite imagery.
To most North Koreans, the life lived by the Kims and others who support the regime would seem not just extravagant but unimaginable. Especially because state propaganda is dedicated to presenting the "supreme leader" as a selfless man who always puts the nation first.
"Kim Jong Un is supposed to be a &#x27;leader of the people&#x27;," Ri Jong Ho says, "yet he has four luxury vessels like these while ordinary people are struggling just to get one meal a day."
Why Kim Jong Un never talks about his mother - or her controversial bloodline
Kim Jong Un was meant to be their only idol - then North Koreans discovered K-pop
Could Kim&#x27;s teen daughter become North Korea&#x27;s next leader?

</details>

### 54. ★★☆☆☆ Rare pink diamond goes on display in France 100 years after amateur heist

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T10:46:41+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The French royal jewel will be on public display for the first time since it was found hidden in an apple after a 1926 theft.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image source, LâÃcole des Arts Joailliers, Benjamin Chelly
Image caption, The nine-carat, pear-shaped gemstone was one of few royal jewels to survive the French Revolution
A rare pink diamond is going on display in France for the first time since it was stolen almost 100 years ago to the day - the last time it was shown to the public.
The nine-carat, pear-shaped gemstone known as the "Grand CondÃ©" was pilfered in an overnight raid on the ChÃ¢teau de Chantilly in October 1926 and concealed in a hollowed-out apple.
The diamond - one of few royal jewels to survive the French Revolution - was recovered a few weeks later when a hungry maid at the hotel in which the thieves were staying took a bite of the apple.
It will once again be on display in the CondÃ© Museum at the ChÃ¢teau de Chantilly until 3 January.
The museum&#x27;s director Mathieu Deldicque said that while the apple story may sounds far-fetched, it was widely covered by newspapers at the time.
Deldicque has faced questions about whether the exhibition may be tempting fate, especially given the string of heists on European cultural institutions in the past 12 months, including the daring daylight robbery of jewellery from the Louvre museum in Paris.
While he declined to reveal to CNN how much the diamond was insured for, he noted that extensive academic study into the properties of the diamond had taken place to make it uniquely identifiable.
The results of this analysis, as well as previously unseen archive material about the gem&#x27;s history and theft, form part of the exhibition, according to the museum.
Naturally occurring pink diamonds are exceptionally rare and often sell for tens of millions of dollars. The highest price ever fetched for one at auction was $71.2m (Â£53.6m) in 2017, according to Sotheby&#x27;s.
The exact origins of the Grand CondÃ© remain a mystery.
It was first mentioned in 1740 in an inventory of Louis Henri de Bourbon, Prince of CondÃ©, drawn up following his death, the ChÃ¢teau de Chantilly said.
It then became part of the collection of Queen Marie-AmÃ©lie of Bourbon-Sicily, the wife of French King Louis Philippe I, and then her son, Prince Henri of OrlÃ©ans, the Duke of Aumale.
He bequeathed it to the Institut de France, which now owns the ChÃ¢teau de Chantilly, 50km (31 miles) north of Paris, along with thousands of artworks and manuscripts which became the MusÃ©e CondÃ© collection.

</details>

- **发生了什么**: <a href="https://news.google.com/rss/articles/CBMizwFBVV95cUxNNldDdnppMXFuUXl5TTNlQ01ZN193R0I2ZFNSYkxIc1dNRktRSDNReWp2UnFYZEhtbG1UY0NmeW5WbFM4M3NSRkRoX3lvQURPZVN0NDdvdEFmVl9jTndydDhvTUsya0pMMmRQQlUyWGhud2tYREhOLUtlblBZUUVfMUdJTzlxRkZtRHI1ekxlS0xWWWhTa3hGVWZnakxnTERPb3NpMktGS3FmQVkxcjRaaFlmSmhBUVZKaEZKVGFYcGdoMEh5U0RwcjE2QUhlS2PSAdQBQVVfeXFMTVRMT2tVaDBEaE5UenlWUS05b21qR1VIVGVJQ0FxSlhJVEFKWjJneUJaLVNGdDlqVEtxY2JhY0JKUlRGU1l4RW94dUVLRnhicl9tT2NoMl9Ob1dHa3hxUFVLTllZQmExQTlTd2duVVFsOVZkdDh2eUp3ZFVFdX

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Northern Oil and Gas completed a buyback program announced in July 2024, retiring 6,656,391 shares, or 6.51% of its equity, for US$172.66 million, including 667,989 shares purchased in the quarter ended September 30, 2026.
The finished repurchase reduces share count while Northern Oil and Gas is being framed as a value stock with a P/E of 7.22 and a P/B of 1.01. This may sharpen investor focus on how cash returns interact with its non operated, multi basin growth model.
The focus now turns to how Northern Oil and Gas&#x27; completed buyback might influence the broader investment narrative around value and capital allocation.
Stack your watchlist with more potential value stories by comparing Northern Oil and Gas to our curated set of 33 high quality undervalued stocks .
To own Northern Oil and Gas, you need to be comfortable with a non operated, multi basin model that leans on acquiring long dated, lower decline assets while carrying meaningful debt and non cash accounting swings. The core near term catalyst is execution on that portfolio, keeping production, costs and free cash flow aligned with acquisition spending.
The completed US$172.66 million buyback slightly tightens the share count, but it does not change the main operational swing factors. The biggest immediate risk remains pressure on reported earnings from impairments, hedge marks and interest costs, which can mask underlying cash generation and limit flexibility for future dividends or further repurchases.
The most relevant recent development for this buyback conversation is the finished repurchase of 6,656,391 shares, equal to 6.51% of Northern Oil and Gas equity, under the program announced in July 2024. That is a sizable cash outlay for a company that already relies on higher cost notes and has interest payments that are not well covered by current earnings.
For investors, this matters because capital returns now sit alongside bond coupons, maintenance spending and new deals, competing for the same cash pool. If operating assets deliver the steady volumes and margins associated with the Utica, Duvernay and ground game portfolio, the reduced share count can increase the impact of results on a per share basis. However, any squeeze on free cash flow could limit the company’s flexibility relatively quickly.
What The Analyst Scenario Implies For Northern Oil and Gas
Analysts sketch out a version of Northern Oil and Gas where higher margins and steadier output do most of the heavy lifting. The forecasts assume revenue expands by 8.2% a year over the next three years, with profit margins moving from a loss position today to 19.6% by 2029. That path would take earnings from a loss of US$486.0 million today to a profit of US$498.5 million by about October 2029, a swing of roughly US$985 million.
Northern Oil and Gas&#x27; narrative projects revenue of US$2.5b and earnings of US$498.5 million by 2029. This assumes 8.2% yearly revenue growth and an earnings swing of about US$985 million from a loss of US$486.0 million today.
Under that consensus view, the stock screens on a P/E of 10x those 2029 earnings, using a discount rate close to 7.9% to bring that scenario back to today. That multiple sits below the current 12.3x P/E quoted for the wider US oil and gas group. This is one reason some investors frame Northern Oil and Gas as a value idea rather than a pure growth story.
There is still a wide band of opinion. The most optimistic analysts cluster around a US$38.00 target, while the most cautious sit at US$25.00, against a current share price of US$23.83 as of mid October 2026. That spread reflects different views on how reliably the non operated, multi basin model can translate into the US$2.5b revenue and US$498.5 million earnings outcome by 2029, and how much weight to place on leverage, impairments and hedge marks along the way.
Uncover why Northern Oil and Gas&#x27; fair value indicates a 12% potential upside to its current price, a discount that could narrow quickly if sentiment shifts.
NYSE:NOG 1-Year Stock Price Chart Exploring Other Perspectives
One alternate view leans heavily on regulatory and decarbonization risk for Northern Oil and Gas. Under that frame, the most bearish analysts tied their US$25.00 target to slower revenue assumptions, roughly US$2.3b by 2029 and earnings of US$622.8 million, set before this buyback update. Use that gap in outlooks to pressure test your own thesis.

### 56. ★★☆☆☆ High interest rates aren’t slowing the AI boom. That’s a problem for the Fed. - Inquirer.com

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: GN Fed

- **发生了什么**: High interest rates aren’t slowing the AI boom. That’s a problem for the Fed.&nbsp;&nbsp;Inquirer.com

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

High interest rates aren’t slowing the AI boom. That’s a problem for the Fed.
There is little sign that higher borrowing costs are doing much to slow artificial intelligence development, which includes spending on chips, data centers, and the electrical systems to power them.
Construction on the Meta data center for artificial intelligence in northeast El Paso, Texas, on July 28, 2026. While many economists think that AI technology could eventually improve productivity, for now the data center boom may be contributing to inflation. Read more Paul Ratje / New York Times
by Colby Smith and Ben Casselman , New York Times Published  Oct. 17, 2026, 5:01 a.m. ET
The Federal Reserve has a conundrum on its hands as it tries to tame elevated inflation. One of the primary drivers of today’s growth, and the price pressures that have followed in its wake, appears nearly immune to the higher interest rates that the central bank has begun to impose on the economy.
Companies’ seeking to expand their artificial intelligence abilities have been undeterred by not only U.S. borrowing costs that have recently reached multi-decade highs, but also soaring costs for electricity, high bandwidth memory, and other inputs that are crucial to continued growth.
The implications for the Fed are vast, if price pressures do not ease as many policymakers expect in the coming months. To return inflation to the Fed’s 2% target, the central bank might need to tighten the screws on the economy more than otherwise would be the case to sufficiently slow activity. The brunt of that adjustment will fall predominantly on industries more sensitive to higher rates, such as housing and the automotive sector, and in turn the people employed by the companies in those fields. Depending on how much the Fed ends up needing to choke off demand, the labor market, which is already starting to cool, could start to crack.
“The problem for the Fed is that there is usually a built-in correction mechanism in the U.S. economy in which interest rates rise and at some point, the rate-sensitive parts of the economy, led by housing, slow down hard, and that then propagates to the rest of the economy,” said Ajay Rajadhyaksha, global chair of research at Barclays. “But if a large part of the economy is just less rate sensitive and that is what is pushing the economy to grow faster, then the Fed, unfortunately, has to hurt the part of the economy that is more rate sensitive.”
The scale of the AI build-out is so immense that it is measured in numbers usually associated with government spending on national defense or healthcare, not private-sector investments. In a recent paper, Stijn Van Nieuwerburgh, an economist at Columbia University, estimated that spending on AI chips, data centers, and the electrical systems to power them would exceed $10 trillion from 2025 to 2032. That is more than 3.6% of total U.S. economic output each year.
A growing share of that investment is being paid for with borrowed money, which in theory should make it responsive to rising interest rates. So far, however, there is little sign that higher borrowing costs are doing much, if anything, to slow the boom.
“If it’s a real transformational technology that potentially is going to define the tech space for years to come; if you have the balance sheet capacity to borrow to make these investments; and if you think the investments are going to be profitable and generate double-digit returns on invested capital, then it doesn’t actually make a huge difference if you’re borrowing at 5.5% or 6%,” said Andrew Sheets, global head of fixed income research at Morgan Stanley. “That 50 basis points is not the limiting factor in whether or not the math works.”
Businesses invested more than $100 billion in computers and related equipment in the second quarter of the year, up 60% from a year earlier and more than 1.5 times the rate in early 2024. Construction of data centers, not counting the chips inside them, has more than quintupled since the beginning of 2022.
The surge in spending reflects in part rapidly rising prices for chips and related equipment. But even adjusting for inflation, investment is growing at a breakneck pace, and shows no sign of slowing.
AI investments have so far been impervious to higher rates and soaring costs, Van Nieuwerburgh said, because other bottlenecks, such as delays in regulatory approvals, are making it hard to build enough data centers to meet exploding demand.
“Currently, we’re in a building crunch where the key problem is we cannot build these data centers fast enough,” he said. If a project gets the necessary approvals, secures the required power, and clears other hurdles, he said, “at that point, you’re going to build this data center pretty much no matter what it costs.”
Higher borrowing costs might lower the return an investor can make on a project, he added, “but not that much, and it’s still going to be fantastic.”
AI investments might not remain immune to higher rates forever. If the supply of data centers catches up with demand, or if the technology fails to live up to its promise and demand cools, then costs will begin to matter again. Even now, there are hints that investors are reconsidering some more speculative projects that do not have firm buyers lined up.
Many economists are also optimistic that, over the long term, AI could improve productivity, allowing the economy to grow more quickly without causing inflation. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh is one of several officials who have said they believe that the technology could ultimately help keep inflation in check.
For now, though, the AI boom seems to be contributing to inflation, not taming it. And it is doing so at a time when forces even more outside the Fed’s control — most notably, the war with Iran — are also pushing up prices for energy and other goods.
Prices for business computing equipment, including chips, were up 11.4% in the second quarter from a year earlier, according to the Bureau of Economic Analysis. Those higher prices are spilling over far beyond the AI industry, affecting other businesses and also consumers. Consumer prices for computing equipment were up 8.4% in August. And this year, Apple said it would raise prices on Macs and iPads because of the soaring cost of memory chips.
The data center building boom is also contributing to the increase in prices for aluminum, copper, and other materials, which were already being pushed up by President Donald Trump’s tariffs. And demand for electricians and other workers involved in data center construction is driving up labor costs. Average hourly earnings of electrical contractors were up 7.1% in August from a year earlier. That is adding to the cost of building factories, office buildings, and apartments.
The problem for the Fed is that it has only a blunt instrument at its disposal. It can’t raise interest rates on certain industries and not others. And if the AI boom is impervious to the effects of higher rates, then other sectors will inevitably bear the bulk of the pain.
Adding to the Fed’s challenge, the sector that is usually hardest hit by rising rates, the housing market, was languishing long before mortgage rates recently surged to their highest level in three years. The residential construction sector is smaller today, adjusted for inflation, than it was before the pandemic, even as the economy as a whole has grown significantly.
As a result, it is not clear that additional rate increases will do much to slow housing further.
“The additional restraint you’re going to get from the housing market is limited,” said Nathan Sheets, a former official at the Treasury Department who is now global chief economist at Citigroup. The sizable portion of Americans who locked in ultralow mortgages when rates plummeted to zero roughly six years ago are unlikely to be swayed to move, unless by necessity, ensuring that home sales remain lackluster. New construction has also yet to pick up, but instead of leading to jobs losses, builders are shifting their focus to data centers.
Construction spending solely for data centers reached an annual rate of $85 billion in August, more than double the rate of two years ago, according to data from the Census Bureau.
There are other channels the Fed can use, but they are less potent than housing typically has been. Monthly payments on automobile loans have become more costly as rates have risen. So too have credit card debt payments. This is likely to keep pressure on lower-income consumers, who already have depleted financial cushions.
Consumer spending has remained robust largely because Americans, especially those in high income brackets, have benefited from a seemingly relentless rally in the U.S. stock market that has been driven primarily by AI companies. The question is how hard the Fed wants to lean against that.
Warsh last month described the Fed’s approach to raising rates as removing a “dose of accommodation,” leaving economists guessing not only how much more tightening might be expected, but also how Warsh would gauge when enough restraint had been imposed on the economy.
“In order to cool the broader economy down — not just the investment outside of AI that’s interest rate-sensitive — you probably need the equity markets to also cool off,” said Tiffany Wilding, an economist at Pimco.
Most of the run-up in equity prices can be attributed to AI-related companies. Any sell-off is likely to disproportionately hit companies outside that sphere, many of whom have seen far more muted gains, or outright losses, this year. Depending on the magnitude, that could eventually pose a risk for the labor market, Wilding said.
“CEO confidence is certainly related to their own equity market prices,” Wilding said. “If you have stock prices going down, CEOs see that and then there’s more of an incentive for them to rightsize their businesses. That’s when you get a potential labor market adjustment.”
This article originally appeared in the New York Times .

How day of protests unfolded as disorder breaks out across France
How day of protests unfolded as disorder breaks out across France Close
An estimated 550,000 people took part in protests across France, demanding greater investment in education and action on the rising cost of living.
The demonstrations quickly descended into violence, with cars and buildings set alight and shops ransacked.
At least 1,200 people were arrested on Saturday as riot police deployed tear gas and water cannons to disperse crowds.
The unrest follows weeks of student-led protests, with demonstrators joining forces to express growing anger over economic conditions and French President Emmanuel Macron handling of the crisis.
In a social media post, Macron denounced the violence and said that vandals and looters will be "prosecuted and punished".
BBC correspondent Adina Campbell reports from Paris.
